This Sustainability Report adheres to the requirements of the EU Non-Financial Reporting Directive 
(NFRD). It also reflects the guiding principles of international organisations and frameworks such as IFRS, 
GRI, Sustainability Accounting Standards Board (SASB), the United Nations Global Compact (UNGC), 
and the United Nations Sustainable Development Goals (UN SDGs).

This report has been prepared under Luxembourgish law transposing the Non-Financial Reporting 
Directive as Luxembourg has not yet transposed the Corporate Sustainability Reporting 
Directive (CSRD).

ArcelorMittal Sustainability Report 2025

## Executive statement

Significant investment in the energy transition
Environmental issues have been important for the steel 
industry for many decades, but in 2006, when ArcelorMittal 
was founded, concern over climate change was not yet 
a mainstream business issue. Although the first global 
climate agreement, the United Nations Framework 
Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC), was adopted in 
1992, the major turning point came in 2015 with the adoption 
of the Paris Agreement. Like many global companies, 
ArcelorMittal responded, setting ambitious targets and 
developing plans to transition to lower-carbon 
technologies. Our ambition to achieving net zero by 2050 
remains in place, but it is important to acknowledge that 
the reality of the transition has been far less straight-forward 
than initially foreseen, and not just for the hard-to-abate 
sectors like steel. Over the last year we have seen the 
challenges discussed publicly. Major institutions are now 
highlighting the difficulties of limiting the global-average 
temperature increase to 1.5°C above pre-industrial levels, 
and the probability of an overshoot. As I write, there are live 
discussions going on in the EU about the future shape of the 
Emissions Trading System (ETS), with some countries even 
calling for the scheme to be cancelled – something that 
would have been very difficult to imagine even this time last 
year. Concerns around competitiveness, re industrialisation, 
inflation and energy security have understandably moved to 
the centre of the debate, leading to increasing scrutiny of 
the EU ETS as an economic policy tool, not just a climate 
one alone.

Dear stakeholders,
Welcome to ArcelorMittal’s 2025 Sustainability Report.  
It covers a lot, so I hope you will find it interesting 
and informative.

At its core, sustainability is simply about running a good 
business — managing our social and environmental impact 
responsibly, acting with integrity and being a company that 
contributes positively to the communities and economies we 
are part of. Trends may shift, but the expectation that we 
operate with purpose, accountability and long term thinking 
does not. That is why sustainability remains central to how we 
lead, how we invest and how we create value for the future.

This year, in July, we will celebrate our 20th anniversary. 
Furthermore, 2026 marks 50 years since the plant that was 
the genesis of ArcelorMittal, Ispat Indo in Indonesia, was 
founded by our Executive Chairman, Mr Mittal. These are 
meaningful milestones – and ones we are proud of. Building 
and maintaining industrial leadership over multiple decades 
is never guaranteed and speaks to our sustainability in its 
broadest definition.

Improvement with all safety KPIs
Today our two most material sustainability issues are safety 
and climate. Safety of course is not new. A priority since the 
creation of the company, safety is a non-negotiable, core 
value and a fundamental expectation for all who work at 
ArcelorMittal. Over the past year we saw further signs that 
the foundations of our multi year transformation are 
beginning to deliver consistent, improved outcomes across 
the organisation. Leadership expectations became clearer, 
risk management practices were applied more uniformly, 
and process safety work expanded across higher risk 
installations. Contractor safety also received sustained 
attention, with enhanced onboarding and awareness, 
broader implementation of the Life Saving Golden Rules 
certification, and preparation of a strengthened fatality 
prevention standard that will be launched in 2026.

Much has changed in the intervening decades – and the 
pace of change is only likely to accelerate in the future. 
The 2020s have emerged as a defining decade: a time when 
the direction of history shifts in meaningful and lasting ways. 
In some ways that future is clear, in other ways it is more 
uncertain. As I write the conflict in Iran, which has caused 
significant disruption, continues, although it is possible – 
and I fervently hope – that by the time this report is 
published there will be a resolution.

What we can say with confidence is that steel – so strong, 
so flexible, so adaptable – will remain a vital material for our 
world. What evolves is how it is made, where it is made and 
how much of it is consumed. It is worth remembering that steel 
demand has doubled in the last 25 years alone. At the turn 
of the century, a large percentage of steel was still made in 
traditional industrial powers, such as Japan, the US and 
Europe. Today China alone produces over half the world’s 
steel, while many countries in the developed world have 
de-industrialised due to globalisation, technological change, 
policy shifts and environmental and regulatory pressure.

Most importantly, we can see these developments positively 
impacting the safety performance across the group. All the 
KPIs we track improved in 2025 compared with 2024. The 
LTIFR was 0.65 versus 0.70; the recordable injury rate (RIR) 
was 3.85 versus 4.80, and the fatality frequency rate (FFR) 
was 0.014 versus 0.035. Tragically though this rate means 
we did still have fatalities – six in total, four of whom were 
contractors. This, and the nature of our operations, means 
the work is far from complete. A strong culture of continuous 
vigilance, disciplined execution, and relentless focus on 
risk reduction remain essential. But I am confident the 
organisation is galvanised to achieve this and that provided 
we keep up the rigorous focus and momentum we will reach 
our zero-fatality goal.

That said, the energy transition is under way and making real 
progress. Renewables have passed the economic tipping 
point and are now often the cheapest source of new 
electricity generation. Electric vehicles also are now cheaper 
to own in most countries when operating costs are included, 
although still slightly more expensive on average to buy. 
When it comes to heavy industry though, the technologies 
are not yet economically competitive and scalable.

The concept of sustainability, and what our stakeholders 
expect in terms of sustainability, has also evolved over the past 
two decades, moving in and out of the spotlight, sometimes 
celebrated, sometimes questioned. For ArcelorMittal, its 
importance has never depended on fashion or external pressure.

## Executive Statement continued

We are looking at the energy transition holistically, seeking to 
contribute and create value not only through decarbonising 
our own operations, but also providing materials and solutions 
for other sectors and expanding into new business areas such 
as renewable energy. People might be surprised to learn that 
we now have 1.6GW of renewable energy commissioned, 
with another 1.2GW under development (equity basis). We 
have already invested a significant amount into the energy 
transition and will continue in this direction focusing on 
opportunities that meet the dual purpose of accelerating 
the transition and providing an attractive rate of return.

modules into one low-carbon system, representing the 
evolution of steel’s role in construction from passive material 
to active energy infrastructure. We are also energized by the 
opportunity to develop advanced steels for renewables, 
hydrogen and data centre infrastructure, positioning the 
business at the heart of fast growing sectors. Data centre 
developers are under pressure to build faster, operate more 
efficiently and reduce environmental impact. Steel can 
really help them achieve this and we have strengthened our 
offering across nearly all core components including 
structural steels, insulated panels, steel fibres for high load 
flooring and components for racks and backup power.

In today’s fast-moving and unpredictable global environment, 
equipping our people with the skills, agility and foresight to 
navigate change successfully is more critical than ever. For 
twenty years now, the ArcelorMittal University has been central 
to our efforts to continually develop our people, running 
a wide selection of courses. In 2025, we strengthened these 
programmes in partnership with best-in-class educational 
institutions. For me, it is essential that our leaders have the 
opportunity to continue learning with the world’s best 
institutions. Investing in their development is not a luxury — it is 
a strategic necessity. The future will belong to organisations 
whose people can think differently, challenge assumptions 
and adapt with confidence to a fast changing landscape.

lighter than reinforced concrete, thus cutting material use 
and transport emissions. The project targets the highest 
available certification standards and shows what becomes 
possible when materials and design work in harmony.

All that is left for me to do is thank everyone who contributes 
to ArcelorMittal’s enduring success: our board of directors, 
segment CEOs, management committee members, 
leadership teams and indeed all our employees around the 
globe. Every single person makes an important contribution 
to the company, starting with safety. We achieved a lot in 
2025, and I look forward to building on the successes and 
achievements in this, our double anniversary year.

There is also a marked difference in the carbon footprint of our 
group, with absolute emissions having reduced by 47.7% since 
2018 including disposals. It is also noteworthy that the assets 
we have disposed had an average emissions intensity of 
2.5 tCO2e/tcs, compared with 1.79 tCO2e/tcs today. We are 
also advancing where the fundamentals support long term 
investment. A major step forward came with the decision, 
earlier this year, to proceed with the 2 million tonne electric arc 
furnace at Dunkirk — a €1.3 billion transformation supported 
by long term access to low carbon electricity and national 
energy efficiency mechanisms. Once commissioned, this 
should significantly reshape the site’s route based emissions 
profile. You will find the transition plan for the investment in this 
report. Based on projects like this which have reached final 
investment decision, we now expect to reduce emissions 
intensity by up to 10% by 2030. Policy is essential to accelerate 
and enable the economics of decarbonised steelmaking, 
which realistically are likely to remain challenging in the next 
decade. Importantly our world-class R&D team continues 
to invest in the development and testing of technologies 
that are expected to play a critical role in the long-term 
decarbonisation, including carbon capture storage (CCS), 
bio-energy and hydrogen.

Harnessing the transformative power of AI
We are also increasingly harnessing AI as a platform for 
innovation that enables new products, new processes, and 
new ways of solving complex problems. In 2025, we moved 
from standalone tools to integrated systems where AI 
supports enhanced safety performance, improves uptime 
predictability, manages product quality and strengthens 
operational resilience. The potential for transformative 
change is enormous and we are aware we are only at the 
beginning. But all the right steps are being taken to build 
a Cloud-First, Data-Centric, Team-Enabled approach that 
will enable us to fully harness real-time intelligence to help 
make decisions and run our complex, industrial operations 
efficiently and sustainably for the future. A great example is 
the deployment of iSMART to create custom monitoring 
dashboards and alert systems in real time for our tailings 
dams. Tailings dam safety and structural integrity are critical 
and material issues for our mining segment, essential to 
protecting the safety of our local communities and 
employees, and safeguarding the environment from 
pollution and flooding. We are working intensively to ensure 
all our TSFs are structurally sound and safe, and I must take 
this opportunity to thank the members of our Board 
Sustainability Committee who travelled to Serra Azul as part 
of their governance oversight responsibilities, to experience 
first-hand the considerable work that is ongoing to fully 
deconstruct the tailings dam, which has been inactive since 
2012. Together, these achievements show how innovation is 
driving a more resilient, efficient and future ready steel 
industry — one that supports sustainability not just through 
lower emissions, but through smarter materials, safer 
operations and more reliable industrial systems.

New Lux HQ will be icon of sustainable steel 
Before I conclude I would like to highlight progress with 
the construction of our new headquarters in Kirchberg, 
Luxembourg. This rapidly rising building will become an icon 
to the sustainability of steel. Its exoskeleton is made from 
XCarb® RRP steel produced at ArcelorMittal Differdange 
and Belval from 100% recycled scrap and 100% renewable 
electricity, then fabricated at our Steligence® centre. 
It demonstrates how circularity and low-carbon steel can 
transform the performance and footprint of a building: 
333 kg CO2/t beams, and a structure seven to ten times

Innovation is our bread and butter
Innovation really is our bread and butter, and core to our 
purpose of “Smarter steels for people and planet.” We 
invested US$335 million in R&D in 2025 across sustainable 
product development, e-mobility, clean energy 
applications, decarbonisation, environmental performance 
and AI. The result was 38 new products launched – all of 
which accelerate sustainable lifestyles and sustainable 
construction. One of my personal favourites is Helioroof®, 
which integrates steel roofing, insulation and photovoltaic

Our business  
model

We are transforming how steels are made and used. While the world needs more steel, a sustainable world needs smarter and decarbonised steels. For ArcelorMittal it means using innovative processes to make 
cleaner and stronger steels that use less energy and emit notably less carbon but also applying the intelligence of our R&D innovation teams to enhance steel’s properties and functionality. We are driven by an 
entrepreneurial spirit, a passion for excellence, with innovation at the heart of our operations.

Chapter 9 
Planet – Safeguarding biodiversity and ecosystems

Fos-sur-Mer. The Fos-Sur-Mer plant has implemented 
a multi-year investment programme to strengthen its 
environmental performance. A central component of this 
programme is the deployment of the Zero Dust at Sinter 
initiative. The installation of two advanced filtration units 
have taken dust emissions from the associated stacks 
consistently largely below the regulatory limits. The plant 
also completed a EUR150 million modernisation of the 
coke plant, including the full refurbishment of its 126 
furnaces. The cumulative effect of these investments has 
been a substantial reduction in key atmospheric emissions 
between 2015 and 2025.

by deploying iSMART (Integrated Surveillance and

cloud-based, GIS-enabled system that incorporates 
multiple data sources to create custom monitoring 
dashboards and alert systems in real time for our 
tailing dams.

## – Case study: Between February and July 2025,

ArcelorMittal Andrade in Brazil conducted a Human 
Rights Due Diligence (HRDD) assessment to identify and 
address potential and actual human rights impacts of its 
operations. The process included planning, regulatory 
analysis, impact assessment through research, interviews, 
surveys, and site visits, and sharing results with stakeholders. 
Engagement involved employees, contractors, local 
communities, authorities, NGOs, and vulnerable groups, 
with 170 participants contributing. Initial findings 
identified areas for improvement, and a follow-up study is 
underway to refine actions. The case demonstrates how 
structured HRDD processes, stakeholder engagement, 
and continuous improvement can be effectively 
embedded into day-to-day site operations.

People
Driving our safety 
transformation

Safety roadmaps
Safety roadmaps are at the heart of our transformation 
programme. These are bespoke action plans that have 
been developed for every single ArcelorMittal segment 
worldwide and are aimed at achieving a group-wide 
aligned approach. Every segment and operational site 
is required to maintain its own roadmap, with smaller 
sites covered through cluster-level plans.

Progress in 2025
We are now over one year into delivering our roadmaps 
and three-year safety transformation programme, with 
visible progress on all key safety performance indicators. 
A key improvement was a reduction of more than 50% in the 
fatality frequency rate (FFR) year-on-year (yoy), resulting 
in the lowest rate recorded to date for ArcelorMittal. Our 
mining operations also achieved an important milestone 
with zero fatalities in 2025. In addition, the group’s lost time 
injury frequency rate (LTIFR) reduced by 7% year-on-year, 
reflecting continued improvement in day-to-day safety 
performance. However, to provide us with a wider view of 
our safety progress, and to include the restricted work 
injuries (the person is still at work but cannot perform his 
usual work) and medical aid injuries (injuries for which a 
person is able to perform their usual work but required

a treatment by a physician), we are also looking at our 
recordable injury rate. We are targeting to show a sustained 
reduction in the recordable injury rate (including contractors)
by 25% vs 2024, which represents 3.6 in 2028. This will be an 
important step in supporting our ambition of zero fatalities 
and serious injuries.

In addition, we are receiving clear feedback from our 
employees that they see and feel the increased focus 
on safety. ArcelorMittal’s employee survey programme, 
Speak Up +, regularly asks employees about their 
experiences, aspirations, and concerns within the business, 
including safety. The results have shown steady progress 
in the perception among employees regarding the 
importance of a strong safety culture and higher levels 
of leadership engagement.

Safety at our company is about people. Our ambition is that 
everyone who works with us returns home safely, every day. 
Building a world-class safety culture requires care, respect 
and dignity for our workforce, combined with disciplined 
safety risk management.

Across our company, more than 130 roadmaps are currently 
in place, comprised of over 3,000 individual actions. These 
actions are organised under five core dimensions which 
all tie to the recommendations from the dss+ safety audit 
which was completed in October 2024:

We are developing one safety culture – all leaders, 
employees and contractors behind shared behaviours, 
aligned systems, and Visible-Felt Leadership. Our safety 
transformation programme is anchored in two pillars:  
One Safety Culture and Safety Risk Management.

(i.e., 2025), we strengthened the foundations of a safer

Progress is monitored through a structured reporting and 
review cycle. Each month, local health and safety (H&S) 
teams update a central tracking tool to record progress 
against their actions, and site heads meet to reinforce 
priorities, identify gaps, and resolve challenges. Segment 
H&S teams conduct periodic site inspections during the 
year to review progress and provide formal updates to 
the segment CEO. Annually, each roadmap undergoes 
a comprehensive review to assess what is working well, 
where further reinforcement is needed, if new actions 
need to be introduced, and what resources are required.

As we move into the second year of our transformation 
journey, our focus is on further embedding the foundations 
established in 2025 by strengthening execution discipline, 
supporting more consistent application of our safety 
approach across all regions, and reinforcing key enablers 
such as leadership capability, process safety, construction 
safety and contractor integration. In parallel, we are working 
closely with our Joint Ventures to align safety expectations, 
strengthen collaboration and support implementation of 
our safety framework across our joint operated assets. 
Over the coming years, we intend to continue scaling these 
efforts, enhancing our assurance processes, and integrating 
safety considerations more deeply into core business 
functions. Through these actions, we aim to support 
sustained improvement in safety performance and 
continued progress towards our ambition of zero fatalities 
and serious injuries.

## People – Driving our safety transformation continued

Together, these elements establish a minimum baseline 
for performance, which business units enhance through 
their site‑specific roadmaps aimed at continuously 
reducing the number of incidents that have the potential 
to lead to serious injuries and fatalities.

These are important milestones, but we are fully aware that 
our journey to strengthen safety performance is far from 
complete. Relentless effort and vigilance remain essential. 
In 2025, we were deeply saddened by the loss of six 
colleagues in five fatal events in India, South Africa, Mexico, 
Bosnia and Venezuela. The causes of the fatalities included 
burns from a hot substance (accounting for fatalities in two 
separate PSM events), falling from height, being crushed by 
mobile equipment, and electrical safety. We performed a 
thorough review of every incident and have embedded the 
findings into the risk management process, which focuses 
on these top safety risks.

## CASE STUDY

## Contractor safety management in Europe

The corporate H&S team leads the development and 
maintenance of group‑wide safety policies and standards 
and designs key performance indicators per management 
level, across all sites and segments. In parallel, the Global 
Health and Safety Council – comprising H&S leaders from 
across the organisation – facilitates the sharing of learnt 
lessons and best practices and promotes our One Safety 
Culture and governance. The Council meets monthly 
virtually and convenes at one of our sites once a year. 
Once a quarter, there is an additional council meeting 
extended to COOs and CEOs.

In 2025, ArcelorMittal Europe launched a strengthened 
contractor safety management approach built around six 
pillars designed to improve collaboration, support cultural 
alignment, and reduce contractor-related risks across all 
sites. These six pillars are:

## – Just and fair culture

A harmonised sanction policy was implemented in 2025, 
supported by clear expectations on rule compliance.  
A positive recognition programme is planned for 2026 to 
highlight contractors demonstrating proactive and safe 
behaviours, reinforcing a balanced approach of both 
accountability and recognition.

## –Transparent H&S communication

Four of the colleagues involved were contractors. As a result, 
contractor safety continues to be an area of heightened 
focus for ArcelorMittal. During 2025, we took steps to build a 
more consistent baseline of expectations across our company, 
rolling out Life-Saving Golden Rules certification to 
contractors and a new Fatality Prevention Standard (FPS) for 
contractor management is planned for launch in H1 2026.

A more structured engagement model has been 
introduced. This includes a letter from top management 
about the fatality prevention standards, annual 
in-person meetings involving procurement, contractors 
and segments at CEO level, and quarterly meetings  
with high-risk contractors to maintain alignment and 
clear expectations.

## – H&S KPIs are integrated into procurement

Contractors are classified green, yellow, red, or 
blacklisted based on safety performance criteria. 
Contractors rated red undergo a formal review every  
six months. These reviews inform procurement decisions 
and support continuous improvement while promoting 
higher safety performance standards.

Accountability for cultivating a strong H&S culture rests with 
the CEOs of each business unit. They are responsible for the 
effective implementation of the SMS within their operations 
and for delivering safety performance, including the delivery 
of their safety roadmaps.

## –Structured collaboration through partnership

This new standard will provide clearer minimum 
expectations for contractor engagement across all sites, 
supported by a structured traffic light assessment of 
contractor safety behaviours and an enhanced approach 
to managing non-compliance with ArcelorMittal policies or 
repeated underperformance. It will also establish formal 
joint audits with contractor leadership to reinforce shared 
accountability for safety performance. Where we have 
already identified poor performance, we have taken action.

agreements 
Formalised partnership agreements now guide long- 
term collaboration. These agreements are supported  
by mandatory quarterly reviews, enabling both sides  
to monitor progress, address challenges, and sustain 
shared accountability.

## – Joint audits based on Fatality Prevention

Standards (FPS) 
ArcelorMittal and contractor teams conduct joint audits, 
which accelerate the adoption of best practices and 
improve compliance with the FPS. Red-rated contractors 
are prioritised to support rapid improvement and 
enhanced oversight.

The enhanced three-lines of assurance model rolled 
out in 2025 supports the CEOs in evaluating whether the 
group-wide safety policies and standards are being 
implemented effectively across all their sites. Site and unit 
management hold primary responsibility for Line 1 
assurance, evaluating whether day-to-day safety controls 
and processes are implemented effectively on the ground. 
Line 2 assurance is carried out by segment management 
and key corporate functions, including ArcelorMittal’s H&S 
and the CTO organisation, providing oversight, guidance, 
and challenge to the sites. Line 3 assurance – delivered 
through the independent global assurance function – 
assesses the robustness of safety processes across 
the organisation.

## –Targeted contractors’ awareness

Contractors receive Life-Saving Golden Rules1 sessions 
and tailored onboarding awareness modules for both 
leaders and frontline workers. This helps create 
a consistent baseline of safety competence and  
reinforces a shared understanding of critical risks  
across European operations.

The five core dimensions of the safety roadmaps
The five core dimensions of the safety roadmaps provide 
a comprehensive framework to address cultural, 
operational, technical, and governance-related safety 
risks in a consistent and measurable manner across all 
regions and activities.

1.	 Life-Saving Golden Rules are a set of 10 rules that detail behaviours 
expected of everyone working at an ArcelorMittal site to prevent 
fatalities and serious injuries. They relate to most common  
hazards and risks encountered across our operations.

A.	Governance and assurance
Our company’s Safety Management System (SMS) provides 
the overarching governance framework that guides all 
business units and sites in managing health and safety. 
The system comprises our H&S policy, the Fatality Prevention 
Standards, training programmes, and our Life‑Saving 
Golden Rules.

Findings from the independent Line 3 assurance activity are 
shared with the Board Audit and Risk Committee to support 
continuous improvement. The Board Sustainability 
Committee also discusses group-level safety performance 
on a quarterly basis and convenes additional safety focused 
meetings when required.

## ArcelorMittal employees performing a safety check

ArcelorMittal Sustainability Report 2025

## People – Driving our safety transformation continued

Beginning in 2025, the executive short-term incentive plan 
was linked to the Recordable Injury Rate (RIR), replacing 
the proactive PSIF (Potential Serious Injury and Fatality) 
indicator used in 2024. This change reflects the fact that 
proactive PSIF reporting had reached the point where 
it was no longer driving significant safety performance 
across many entities, and it was deemed that the RIR would 
be a better indicator to continue to drive improvement. 
For 2025, the annual RIR target is set at 93% of the previous 
year’s rate. We also apply a circuit breaker based on the FFR 
which was set at zero for the Executive Office and 0.006 for 
the rest of the company in 2025. From 2026, this has been 
reduced to zero by business unit. Safety performance 
represents 15% of the short term incentive.

 Example: The first two lines of assurance in North America
ArcelorMittal North America has strengthened the 
first two lines of its safety assurance with the third line 
completed by internal audit once the first two 
are complete.

CASE STUDY
CASE STUDY

Visible and engaged 
leadership in Brazil

Calvert Safety 
Culture Committee

Line 1 assurance focuses on verifying that the fatality 
prevention standards are fully implemented and that 
all required operational checks and inspections on the 
presence and effectiveness of risks controls are being 
completed on a shift/daily/weekly/monthly basis. These 
checks are carried out by the site Health and Safety team, 
with outcomes reported to the site COO and CEO. Fatality 
prevention standards self-evaluations are expected to 
happen at least once every two years.

In 2025, the Executive Vice President – President of 
ArcelorMittal Brazil and CEO of Flat South America 
started bringing together groups of leaders from 
different levels for sessions dedicated to developing 
and implementing the segment’s roadmap towards 
achieving zero fatalities and serious injuries. More 
than 600 supervisors, managers, GMs and VPs 
participated in 2025, creating a space for open 
dialogue on health and safety. Each session includes 
a deep dive into corporate guidance, sharing safety 
experiences, and open discussion.

The Calvert Safety Culture Committee was 
established in 2023 to support the site’s Culture and 
Risk Management roadmap to accelerate the shift 
from a rules-based, dependent safety approach 
toward greater ownership, peer accountability, and 
interdependent behaviours. Leadership selected 
twelve employees with strong influence across the 
site to serve on the committee.

Safety also accounts for 20% of the long-term incentive 
for the Executive Office and 15% for the Executive Officers, 
increased from 10% in 2024 to reinforce our company’s focus 
on safety. The long-term incentive is based on the FFR. For 
2025 grants, the long-term incentive for the Executive Office 
and Executive Officers is based on achieving an FFR of zero.

The Line 2 assurance process independently verifies that 
Line 1 is effective and that required safety practices are 
being followed in accordance with corporate guidance. 
This is achieved through on‑site checks that confirm 
critical tools, processes, and safety rituals are in place 
and being used correctly. During the Line 2 audit, 
evidence such as documentation, policies, permits, 
training records, and photographs of good practice and 
improvement opportunities is gathered directly from the 
shop floor. Line 2 audits are carried out by the Segment 
Health and Safety Director with the finding reported to 
the Segment CEO. Every site is required to complete a 
Line 2 audit at least once every 24 months.

The committee’s membership rotates annually 
to bring in fresh perspectives, and its insights are 
shared across the site to reinforce continuous 
improvement. This structure ensures that learnings 
are consistently embedded into daily operations 
and that the committee remains a dynamic driver 
of cultural progress.

Every session concludes with all leaders signing a 
pledge to reaffirm their personal commitment to the 
segment roadmap. Participants are given a group 
photo and a message from the EVP encouraging them 
to cascade the discussion to their teams, supporting 
alignment across all levels of the segment.

B. Leadership competencies
Strong leadership accelerates organisational maturity, 
eliminates hazards, and rigorously applies safer working 
practices. In 2025, 80 of the top leaders completed an 
eight-month coaching programme about leading safely. 
The training included a special focus on safety governance 
and impact with workers on the shop floor.

Over the past three years, the Calvert Safety Culture 
Committee has advanced Calvert’s safety culture 
through a wide range of initiatives – from analysing 
past incidents to identifying trends, to developing 
programmes that better integrate contractors into 
the ArcelorMittal safety culture, and highlighting 
examples of strong safety performance across the 
site. For example, the committee has played a key 
role in recognition and engagement initiatives, and 
ongoing communication efforts designed to keep 
safety visible and relevant. It introduced a series of 
structured recognition programmes designed to 
strengthen safety ownership and engagement. 
These include annual awards for the safest mill, 
individual employees for going ‘above and beyond’ 
on safety, and for standout safety projects.

Separately, leaders in Flat South America are also 
celebrating safe behaviour on the shop floor through 
a new internal social platform that allows employees 
and managers to publicly acknowledge positive 
safety behaviours in real time. Leaders can either 
provide written recognition or they are equipped with 
points that they can award to team members who 
demonstrate proactive safety actions, reinforcing the 
message that safe behaviour is noticed and valued. 
The points can be exchanged for restaurant vouchers 
or other benefits. This simple but powerful mechanism 
is strengthening trust, visibility, and daily 
engagement on the shop floor.

Complementing this is a new H&S competency model 
for leaders that was launched in 2025. It emphasises 
three principles: leadership as the foundation for safety, 
Visible-Felt Leadership3 as a daily practice, and a deep 
understanding of safety fundamentals. Leaders are 
expected to set the tone through their presence, behaviour, 
and decisions, recognising the powerful influence they have 
on culture and team motivation. They must also master key 
H&S concepts – hazard identification, risk analysis, and 
robust controls that account for human factors – to support 
safe working conditions for employees and contractors.

Furthermore, to ensure best practices are shared, site 
health and safety officers are encouraged to participate 
in Line 2 audits at other sites within North America. This 
ensures continued development within the North America 
health and safety teams.

In 2025, we also strengthened the alignment between 
executive remuneration and health and safety performance 
by revising the safety related KPIs used in both short-term 
and long-term incentive plans.

3.	 Visible-Felt Leadership (VFL) represents a transformative approach 
to leadership, emphasising the power of presence and the tangible 
impact leaders have on their teams. Leaders inspire and motivate 
team members by visibly and consistently demonstrating 
high standards.

While these controls are grounded in the FPS, sites have

## ArcelorMittal employee in coil yard

Verification processes – such as daily, shift‑based, 
or pre‑task critical control checklists – were further 
standardised in 2025. These tools support confirmation 
that all critical controls are in place and functioning as 
intended before work proceeds.

Going forward, as leaders seek to progress in their careers, 
they will be evaluated on their ability to foster a strong safety 
culture and uphold the H&S expertise essential for protecting 
people, assets, and regulatory compliance.

In 2025, sites increased the deployment of ‘Red Scorpion’ 
oversight (independent safety stewards who provide 
real‑time assurance of high‑risk work) during periods of 
high-risk activity, supporting stronger operational discipline 
and more consistent risk control. Their presence on the shop 
floor reinforces frontline leadership and supports continuous 
verification of FPS requirements, critical controls, and 
Life‑Saving Golden Rules. They are authorised to intervene, 
correct deviations, and stop work where safety may 
be compromised.

C.	Occupational risk management
Occupational risk management is central to preventing 
serious injuries and fatalities. In 2025, we focused on the 
consistent application of our Fatality Prevention Standards 
(FPS) and the verification of critical controls before high-risk 
work. Our FPS define the minimum requirements for managing 
activities with a high risk of causing serious injuries or fatalities 
across all operations. The FPS are a central element of our 
group-wide SMS and a key mechanism for preventing 
severe incidents.

To support consistent deployment, all sites or clusters have 
now established Local FPS Committees. Throughout 2025, 
these committees contributed to strengthening governance 
by helping to clarify FPS requirements, supporting their 
effective implementation, and promoting their reinforcement. 
Their work has focused on improving cross‑functional 
collaboration, enhancing worker engagement, and sharing 
lessons learned across the organisation.

D.	Process safety management (PSM)
Process safety is also a foundational element of our 
safety governance. At its core, effective Process Safety 
Management (PSM) supports the prevention of unplanned 
releases of solids, gases, liquids, molten materials and 
energy, the loss of control and or the loss of integrity of our 
critical process – protecting our people, the environment, 
and the continuity of our business.

## CASE STUDY

AI-enabled safety monitoring on the  
ArcelorMittal Steel Service Centres shop floor 
to improve safety practices

All sites continue to provide quarterly updates on FPS 
compliance. These updates now include more structured 
reporting on implementation progress, identified gaps, 
and corrective actions, supporting greater transparency 
and accountability across the organisation.

Our approach to PSM integrates engineering excellence, 
operational discipline, and strong safety leadership. As 
explained in our response to the dss+ safety audit completed 
in September 2024, we have been enhancing the PSM 
management to learn from best in industry methods largely 
coming from petrochemical industries. Our new PSM 
framework launched in 2025, establishes minimum barrier 
requirements (e.g. preventative actions or mitigation actions 
once an event has occurred such as fire suppression) across 
all facilities and places equal emphasis on maintaining 
these barriers to support long-term integrity and reliability. 
This includes systematic monitoring, verification, and 
continuous improvement of the controls that prevent high 
consequence incidents.

underneath the load. If a worker enters this unsafe area, 
the system triggers an immediate audible warning on the 
shop floor. This proactive alerting helps prevent accidents 
before they occur.

ArcelorMittal Steel Service Centres has introduced 
an AI-supported CCTV system across its shop floors to 
strengthen real time safety performance and reduce 
the risk of workplace incidents. The system continuously 
analyses live video feeds and identifies workers, 
equipment, and moving machinery to verify safe 
behaviours and the correct use of personal protective 
equipment such as gloves and helmets.

Critical controls are embedded within the FPS and are 
verified as effective before any high‑risk activities begin. 
During 2025, all sites completed updated risk assessments 
to identify which high‑risk activities are applicable and 
confirm the corresponding critical controls required.

This initiative is designed to empower employees to take 
ownership of safe working practices. By providing instant 
feedback and increasing situational awareness, the 
technology supports a culture of prevention, continuous 
improvement, and shared responsibility for safety.

This framework has embedded safety requirements 
throughout the full project life cycle – from early design and 
engineering through construction and start up – and is aligned 
with the Capital Investment in Projects Methodology (CIPM 2.0).

In 2025, we launched 12 operational pilots to accelerate the 
adoption of structured PSM practices across the company. 
The 12 operational pilots were selected from mining and 
steel operations across regions. Insights and learnings from 
these pilots are being shared widely to build capability, 
strengthen governance, and support the rollout of

## CASE STUDY

## PSM sinter pilot in Pecém, Brazil

To test, validate and refine these requirements, the 
team piloted the minimum safety requirements across 
five active projects at varying stages of development 
and in diverse geographies.

Sintering is an essential step in preparing iron ores for blast 
furnaces. Fine, iron-bearing materials are heated up to 
1,400°C and fused into larger particles known as sinter. 
This process involves combustion, the handling of 
hazardous materials, and the movement of heavy 
equipment – creating conditions where severe process 
safety risks can arise. This makes a sintering plant an 
appropriate environment to apply and test a structured 
PSM approach.

risks. Preventive actions were identified and implemented 
to address these risks, establishing a new baseline for how 
the sinter plant is operated and maintained.

Through these efforts, we are working to embed 
systematic, detailed, and resilient PSM practices across 
all our operations.

This technical work was reinforced by targeted training. 
Around 450 employees participated in training sessions 
that explained how PSM principles support improved risk 
prioritisation, asset integrity, and day‑to‑day operational 
decision‑making.

In 2026, these requirements will be deployed across 
all capital projects under our company’s PMO’s remit, 
supported by structured gap assessment tools and a suite 
of new project safety standards covering fire, electrical, 
equipment, process, and construction safety. A dedicated 
SICP Manual, targeted e-learning, and engineering 
guidance will further clarify expectations and support 
consistent application across regions and project scopes. 
The overarching goal is to strengthen safety performance, 
reliability and project delivery discipline across the capital 
project portfolio.

E.	 Integrating H&S into support business processes
Embedding H&S into the employee lifecycle
From a cultural and behavioural perspective, our corporate 
HR team has been working to integrate H&S into every stage 
of the employee life cycle – from recruitment and onboarding 
to learning, development and performance management. 
In 2025, corporate HR strengthened policies, clarified safety 
leadership expectations and introduced a ‘Just and Fair’ 
culture framework to create individual accountability for 
respecting safety rules and provide recognition of positive 
behaviours. Another important part of this work included 
partnering with our corporate H&S team to embed strong 
safety behaviours and leadership competencies across 
the organisation. These efforts combine to support a more 
consistent, accountable safety culture and provide our 
employees and contractors entering the business with an 
understanding of our expectations on safety. For further 
details on the changes HR has implemented in safety  
during 2025, see chapter 3.

The pilot has strengthened collaboration across teams, 
both within Pecém and with other ArcelorMittal sinter plants 
across the group. A dedicated, group-wide PSM working 
group is now in place to support alignment and accelerate 
learning transfer.

The PSM sinter pilot in Pecém was part of the first group of 
projects launched in 2025 and has already contributed to 
strengthening risk management and improving 
operational reliability.

In the first phase of the pilot, the team conducted an updated 
and detailed Process Hazard Analysis (PHA), providing an 
enhanced understanding of the installation’s process safety

Through this work, the pilot has contributed to a more 
sustainable foundation for process-risk reduction and will 
guide the broader rollout of the PSM framework across all 
ArcelorMittal’s sinter plants through 2026.

Priorities for 2026
In 2026, we will focus on scaling and embedding the 
foundations established in 2025, with particular emphasis 
on contractor integration and consistent execution across 
all regions.

Safety is one of our company’s core values. The whole 
company has a clear, actionable plan on what it needs 
to do to reduce variability in the safety performance and 
is dedicated to demonstrating further progress in year 
two of our transformation programme.

Strengthening safety management throughout

People
Delivering opportunity 
and talent

Developing leadership that inspires excellence
Having strong, inspirational and future-ready leaders is 
essential as we navigate the growing operational complexity 
of a global steel and mining organisation.

As part of our ongoing focus on improving safety performance 
across our company, safety has now been embedded as a 
core component of all people-related policies. In addition, 
we introduced new standards to establish a consistent 
approach to consequence management for safety-related 
behaviours, which is described in more detail below.

## – Developing leadership that inspires excellence

## – Attracting and developing talent to thrive for the future

## – Engaging everyone through equal opportunity, safety

and inclusion.

Leaders play a key role in shaping culture and behaviours, 
particularly in relation to safety, and act as multipliers for 
engagement, innovation, efficiency, and development 
across the organisation. There is increasing recognition that 
interpersonal leadership skills are critical as they enable 
leaders to inspire and influence their teams, build trust, 
strengthen communication, and drive performance. 
Additionally, identifying and accelerating high-potential 
talent remains a priority to strengthen our succession 
pipeline and ensure we have the leadership depth required 
for future business needs.

During 2025, we continued to strengthen these three pillars, 
supported by a strong focus on embedding safety across 
all areas of the business.

During the year, we also undertook a comprehensive review 
of our Diversity & Inclusion Policy, placing greater emphasis 
on non-discrimination, and merit-based opportunity across 
the workforce. As a result, the policy evolved into the Equal 
Opportunity and Non-Discrimination Policy, which is 
scheduled for launch in 2026.

Our People strategy aims to foster a safety-first, 
people-driven culture that encourages inspirational 
leadership, builds talent and opportunity, and delivers 
strong and sustainable performance. As our industry 
continues to face ongoing transformation driven by 
digitalisation, evolving market conditions, and regulatory 
expectations, the capability, engagement, and wellbeing 
of our people remain critical to our long-term success.

Embedding safety throughout the employee journey
Our absolute priority is the safety and wellbeing of our 
people, and HR plays a critical role in shaping the behaviours, 
expectations, and leadership capabilities that make strong 
safety performance sustainable.

The Joint Global H&S Committee continues to identify areas 
for improvement and harmonise safety performance across 
the group. The Committee operates under the agreement 
between the group and the global trade union IndustriALL, 
established in 2008, which recognises the important role of 
trade unions in improving health and safety. Composed of 
16 representatives of management and the unions, it deals 
with issues related to H&S and does not act as a negotiation 
committee on behalf of unions or management. One of its 
primary priorities is centred around overseeing deployment 
and monitoring the compliance of local joint H&S panels. 
This involves developing guidelines to track progress, 
conducting site visits to assess implementation, and offering 
suggestions for improvement. Additionally, the Joint Global 
H&S Committee provides recommendations on transversal 
and global topics to enhance overall safety measures.

We believe that strong safety leadership leads to fewer 
unsafe conditions, supports more predictable safety 
outcomes and helps reduce incidents.

Attracting, developing, and retaining talent enables us 
to innovate, strengthen operational resilience and maintain 
our focus on responsible business practices. Central to this 
is fostering a non-discriminatory environment based on 
merit, where employees feel included, valued and are 
equipped to contribute meaningfully to our purpose of 
creating smarter steels for people and planet. By embedding 
robust policies, strengthening leadership and employee 
development, and continually enhancing our safety 
culture, we are building a workforce fit for the future 
and a strong people foundation for the next phase 
of ArcelorMittal’s growth.

As a result, we continue to invest significantly in leadership 
development through ArcelorMittal University (AMU), ensuring 
leaders of all levels have access to structured development 
opportunities. A key part of this investment is our Leadership 
Pipeline programmes, which provide high-potential leaders 
with a blended approach combining face-to-face and digital 
learning. These programmes equip leaders with critical skills 
while identifying and nurturing future leadership talent.

In 2025, working in close partnership with our health and 
safety teams, we focused on embedding safety more 
comprehensively within people-related policies, practices, 
and procedures across the business.

We also strengthened accountability through the rollout of 
group standards and a framework on Just and Fair Culture, 
ensuring that every site and segment meets minimum safety 
expectations, recognises positive safety behaviours, and 
applies clear and consistent consequences for non-
compliance. Strengthening safety leadership remains 
essential to embedding a consistent, accountable safety 
culture across all sites and functions. To support this, a new 
safety leadership competency model was created to define 
the skills, knowledge, behaviours, and mindset required of 
leaders at all levels.

In 2025, we strengthened these programmes in partnership 
with best-in-class educational institutions, introducing a 
more modular, continuous approach that can be refreshed 
regularly and adapted to evolving business needs. During 
the year, 769 employees graduated from these programmes.

Governance, compliance, and policies
We continuously review and update our people-related 
policies to ensure they remain relevant, effective and 
compliant across the regions in which we operate. These 
policies are designed to deliver positive and consistent 
outcomes for our stakeholders, including improved safety 
performance, reduced compliance risk, stronger alignment 
with global standards, enhanced employee trust and 
fairness, and the development of a future-ready workforce 
that supports long-term growth. At the same time, 
stakeholders increasingly expect greater consistency 
in people-related policies and standards across our 
global operations.

In 2025, alongside two virtual meetings, one in-person 
meeting was held to review the Committee’s effectiveness, 
and the members conducted two site visits to ensure 
progress and compliance on the ground. During the year, 
the Committee’s charter was reviewed and updated to 
further strengthen its governance.

In parallel, building AI fluency across our leadership 
is increasingly critical as digitalisation reshapes how 
we operate, innovate and compete. During the year, 
AI-readiness training programmes were rolled out and 
made available to all senior leaders.

In addition, the Global Resourcing Policy and supporting 
materials have been updated to incorporate key 
requirements on safety behaviours. H&S has also been 
further integrated into our Performance Management 
Guidelines and short-term incentive metrics.

Progress in 2025
Our People Strategy was initially launched in 2022 and is 
anchored in our purpose of creating smarter steels for people 
and planet. It seeks to build a safety-first, people-driven 
culture that supports sustainable performance for our 
business and our stakeholders. The strategy is structured 
around three pillars:

Attracting and developing talent to thrive for the future
Securing and nurturing the right talent is essential to 
delivering our strategic business objectives in an increasingly 
competitive labour market. We aim to strengthen our 
position as an employer of choice by attracting people who 
are motivated by meaningful work and committed to high 
performance. Achieving this requires a culture where 
colleagues feel supported in their professional growth, 
empowered to innovate, and able to contribute to the 
company’s long-term success.

By embedding safety across the employee life cycle, we 
are improving consistency, reducing behavioural risk, and 
ensuring that every employee is equipped with a strong 
foundation in safety expectations.

During 2025, several enhancements were made to our 
policies to reflect evolving regulatory requirements and 
organisational priorities, particularly in relation to safety.

## CASE STUDY

World Steel Association’s ‘Excellence in People’ 
Steelie Award for ArcelorMittal Kryvyi Rih and 
its reintegration programme for Ukrainian army 
veterans and support for families of the fallen

As a result, we improved the way we set yearly objectives 
and ensured that employees in the programme had a 
development plan in place. This progress was reflected in 
the 2025 Speak Up + results, which showed improvements 
in perceptions of career growth and learning opportunities.

Company-wide, we achieved further growth in both the 
quality and quantity of our virtual learning offer through 
AMU. In total, we recorded 929,991 learning hours with 
139,861 AMU active learners during the year, representing 
an average of 5.9 learning hours per learner. Throughout the 
year, there were also 20,236 attendees at AMULive! events, 
our weekly, company-wide learning initiative.

Building on our employee engagement
Speak Up + regularly surveys employees on their 
experiences, aspirations, and concerns within the business. 
Engagement levels are benchmarked against internal 
targets and external industry peers. Based on survey 
outcomes, concrete actions are continuously developed 
and implemented to address employee concerns and 
drive engagement.

This case study demonstrates how we support our  
people through compassionate, thoughtful practices 
and a strong culture of care, even in the most 
challenging environments.

Aligned with the core principles of the group’s People 
Strategy, the programme is designed to rebuild and 
develop veterans’ roles within the business, while creating 
an environment where every employee feels valued, 
protected, and supported in their onward development. 
In parallel, the programme provides access to physical 
rehabilitation and emotional recovery, recognising 
that sustainable performance is closely linked to 
employee wellbeing.

The Thrive development programme continued across North 
America, Brazil and Europe, with increased participation and 
more employees moving internally into new roles, segments, 
functions and locations. The programme supports 
employees by connecting them with opportunities aligned 
with their skills and aspirations, helping colleagues broaden 
their experience while strengthening internal mobility 
across ArcelorMittal.

Launched in 2010, the World Steel Association’s  
Steelie Awards recognise member companies for their 
contribution to the steel industry across a range of 
categories over a one-year period. The Excellence in 
People award specifically recognises outstanding 
efforts in fostering a thriving workforce, creating 
supportive workplaces, building organisational 
capabilities, and driving impactful learning, 
engagement, and transformation.

During the year, our overall engagement score remained 
stable with a high proportion of employees participating 
in the survey. The 2025 results highlight freedom of opinion 
as a clear organisational strength, demonstrating that 
employees increasingly feel safe and empowered to 
express their views.

In addition, ArcelorMittal Kryvyi Rih has taken 
responsibility for supporting more than 200 families 
of fallen employees, as well as families of missing and 
captured workers. An ecosystem of communication, 
mutual support, and care was established, including 
financial assistance and the organisation of dedicated 
support and remembrance initiatives.

Engaging everyone through equal opportunity,  
safety and inclusion 
Creating an inclusive, merit-based environment is 
essential to attracting and retaining talent, enabling high 
performance, and supporting our transformation as a global 
steel and mining business. Our goal is to ensure all employees 
feel safe, are treated fairly and have equal access to 
opportunities based on individual merit. We seek to eliminate 
bias by strengthening our policies, practices, and behaviours, 
while fostering an environment that promotes collaboration, 
mutual respect, compassion, and high performance.

Our group mentoring programme, which is designed to 
provide all our employees with an opportunity to participate 
in a mentoring relationship, recorded 1,027 mentoring hours 
throughout 2025.

In 2025, ArcelorMittal Kryvyi Rih, which continues to 
operate and rebuild amid the ongoing uncertainty 
caused by the war in Ukraine, received the Excellence in 
People award for its highly impactful programme focused 
on the reintegration of army veterans and the support 
of families of fallen employees. The initiative has led to 
the creation of a comprehensive ecosystem for veteran 
reintegration, supporting the gradual return of 3,056 
employees mobilised for war. To date, out of 620 returned 
veterans, 258 have successfully resumed work.

Together, these actions reflect ArcelorMittal’s dedication 
to people-centred practices that strengthen resilience, 
enhance engagement, and support long-term 
organisational success.

Communities are central to our business. They are home 
to our people, a source of the talent and skills that support 
our operations, and the foundation of the environments, 
resources, infrastructure, and social and cultural contexts 
in which we operate. We are determined to act as 
responsible and trusted partners, contributing positively 
to the communities where we operate, and supporting 
mutually beneficial socio-economic development. 
The economic, social, and cultural wellbeing of communities 
is integral to our ability to operate responsibly, manage risk 
effectively, and maintain our licence to operate.

In 2025, ArcelorMittal Mexico advanced the 
implementation of a more strategic, evidence-based 
community engagement model in the Lázaro Cárdenas 
operating area, building on the comprehensive 
socio-economic assessment completed in 2024. The 
region is characterised by a complex social context, 
diverse stakeholder expectations and long-standing 
land tenure sensitivities, requiring sustained and 
structured engagement.

During the year, ArcelorMittal Mexico began 
implementing initiatives directly informed by these 
priorities. These include entrepreneurship awareness 
activities and competitive grants for local ventures; basic 
education completion programmes for women; digital 
inclusion initiatives; and expanded arts and cultural 
activities celebrating regional identity. Together, these 
programmes represent a shift towards interventions 
designed to strengthen productive skills, social mobility 
and community resilience, moving beyond participation 
metrics to measurable outcomes.

knowledge, and tools required to ensure effective and 
consistent implementation across all operations.

During 2025, we continued to advance all areas, with 
a particular focus on strengthening governance frameworks, 
improving data management and decision-making, and 
building social performance capability across the business.

Progress in 2025 
Governance: Strengthening responsible practices
Following the independent human rights saliency 
assessment conducted in 2023, we took several important 
steps to strengthen our governance framework. That same 
year, we updated our Human Rights Policy to better reflect 
alignment with international standards and the priority 
areas identified in the assessment.

The 2024 assessment, led by an independent third party, 
established a robust social baseline across key dimensions 
– including demographic and social conditions, access 
to public services and infrastructure, and local economic 
opportunities and livelihoods. More than 2,000 individuals 
participated through surveys, interviews and focus group 
discussions, with balanced gender representation, 
applying nationally recognised indicators such as the 
Multidimensional Poverty Index and the Social Progress 
Index. This work strengthened the evidence base for 
decision-making and clarified priority needs and 
perceptions of operational impacts.

Respect for human rights is a key principle guiding our 
relationship with communities and is embedded in our 
approach to social performance. We seek to prioritise 
meaningful and inclusive engagement, the identification 
of shared value and sustainable development opportunities, 
and the timely, transparent, and effective management of 
community concerns and grievances.

In parallel, the operation integrated the assessment 
insights into its risk and impact management processes, 
enabling more structured monitoring of social 
performance indicators and clearer alignment with 
corporate sustainability objectives. The team also 
enhanced dialogue mechanisms to ensure continuous 
feedback as programmes are rolled out and refined.

Building on this foundation, in 2024 we enhanced the 
company’s Grievance Procedure, incorporating elements 
consistent with the UNGPs. These enhancements include 
clearly defined escalation pathways, the categorisation of 
grievances according to severity, and explicit provisions 
addressing remediation and outcomes.

Our approach is grounded in the UN Guiding Principles on 
Business and Human Rights (UNGPs) and underpinned by 
a structured, risk-based framework. In 2023, we commissioned 
an independent human rights saliency assessment, 
conducted by recognised external experts, to provide an 
objective evaluation of our most significant human rights, 
social, and environmental impacts across our company.

Looking ahead, additional pilot programmes will 
be launched to test scalable models in priority areas. 
By embedding assessment learnings into governance 
structures and operational planning, the operation is 
strengthening its ability to prioritise material social 
risks and opportunities, adapt to evolving expectations 
and build long-term, trust-based relationships 
with communities.

In 2025, the focus shifted from diagnosis to execution 
and integration. The findings were translated into 
targeted programme design and embedded into 
site-level planning processes. While the assessment 
had identified positive recognition of existing initiatives 
– including cultural programmes and educational 
scholarships – it also highlighted opportunities to 
increase long-term impact, particularly in employability, 
entrepreneurship, public education, infrastructure and 
access to cultural and sports activities.

In 2024 we also undertook a comprehensive revision of our 
External Stakeholder Engagement Procedure to strengthen 
expectations for meaningful, inclusive and rights-respecting 
engagement. The updated procedure was finalised and 
launched in 2025. To support implementation, we delivered 
training to more than 100 people from all segments of the 
business and organised a specialised workshop for 30 key 
employees leading social performance in their entities, 
as part of our ongoing efforts to promote transparency, 
consistency, and high-quality engagement practices.

The assessment identified twelve priority areas requiring 
enhanced focus. Of these, four relate directly to our 
affected communities:

## –Grievance mechanisms and remediation

## –Community engagement

## –Indigenous Peoples’ rights

## –Land access and resettlement

## Community engagement initiative in Mexico

People – Evolving our approach to community engagement and social performance continued

In 2025 we launched a comprehensive guidance on 
conducting Human Rights Due Diligence (HRDD) as part 
of our efforts to promote human rights. It outlines clear 
steps for identifying, assessing, managing, tracking, 
and communicating impacts, including those affecting 
communities and vulnerable groups, and draws on 
international standards and best practices.

During 2025, we advanced our social and community 
governance framework further by preparing two additional 
guidance-development projects focused on salient human 
rights risks in Land Rights and Resettlement, and Indigenous 
Peoples’ Rights. These guidance documents were 
developed to provide clear expectations, minimum 
requirements, and practical tools for operations and are 
planned to be piloted across business segments in 2026.

Land Rights & Resettlement and Indigenous Peoples’  
Rights Guidance
The Land Access, Involuntary Resettlement & Displacement 
Guidance and the Indigenous Peoples’ Rights Guidance were 
developed to strengthen a rights-based, people-centred 
approach across our operations. They serve as frameworks 
that promote continuous learning, accountability, and 
improvement. They translate our corporate commitments 
and international standards into practical actions that help 
us respect people’s rights, protect cultural heritage and 
minimise disruption to land, livelihoods and traditional 
practices. Their purpose is to inform and support our 
operations to plan and implement their activities in ways 
that uphold dignity, fairness, and inclusion – particularly 
for communities and Indigenous Peoples.

The guidance explicitly addresses cultural sensitivities, the 
protection of vulnerable groups, confidentiality, and safe 
engagement practices, with a strong emphasis on enabling 
access to remedy and avoiding the risk of additional harm. 
The manual also clearly defines roles, responsibilities, 
processes, and tools to ensure investigations are conducted 
impartially and in line with ArcelorMittal’s principles of 
responsible community engagement, including non-
discrimination, cultural and gender sensitivity, and free, 
prior and informed consent.

## CASE STUDY

Risk management and disclosure: strengthening systems
To support our efforts to strengthen systems for monitoring, 
reporting, and decision-making in social performance, we 
undertook a comprehensive group-wide assessment of 
social performance practices in 2024. This assessment 
evaluated how our entities identify, assess, and mitigate 
social risks across key areas, including remediation, 
stakeholder engagement, and community investment.

Strengthening approaches to resettlement  
and land rights in Liberia

We designed the guidance documents to follow a clear, 
project life-cycle structure so that teams can apply the 
right actions at the right time, from the earliest stages of 
planning through construction, commissioning, operations, 
monitoring and closure. Each module includes tools, case 
examples, and decision steps that guide teams through 
complex, real-world challenges. This modular structure also 
allows flexibility: operations can use the full process or focus 
on specific areas such as engagement, impact assessment, 
compensation, or livelihood restoration, depending on local 
needs and project maturity.

grounded in free, prior, and informed consent, verified 
asset inventories, and transparent payment mechanisms 
aligned with IFC Performance Standard 5 and Liberian 
regulations. Digital tools such as QR coded Resettlement 
ID Books, geo fenced land take areas, and digital farm 
inventories are improving traceability, reducing disputes 
and fraud, and strengthening accountability. Clear 
baselines and eligibility criteria help manage expectations 
while protecting legitimate land and livelihood rights.

As our operations evolve and expand, access to land and 
the way it is managed can have profound implications for 
communities. Across our business, resettlement and land 
rights have been reaffirmed as salient human rights issues 
– particularly in regions where land tenure systems are 
complex and livelihoods are closely tied to farming. 
Strengthening how we manage these impacts is therefore 
not only a compliance priority, but central to building trust 
and long-term relationships with host communities.

The findings of this assessment have laid the foundation 
for more consistent and comparable social performance 
measurement across operations and informed the 
subsequent development of operational tools to support 
the segments in effectively implementing our policies 
and procedures.

In 2025, we also progressed the development of the 
Social Performance Management Tool, a dedicated digital 
platform designed to provide our global operations with 
structured guidance and practical support in reporting and 
managing social performance matters. The tool integrates 
updated policies and procedures and supports key 
processes, including stakeholder identification and analysis, 
engagement planning, grievance management, and 
performance monitoring. Throughout 2025, the platform 
underwent intensive development and testing during both 
the first and second halves of the year to refine functionality 
and ensure operational relevance. During 2025 we also 
developed a Community Grievance Severity Classification 
Tool. Aligned with the UNGPs, the tool provides a standardised 
framework for categorising grievances by type, severity, and 
stakeholder vulnerability, ensuring grievances are directed 
to the appropriate personnel for investigation and 
resolution. We plan to launch the tool in 2026 through a 
user-friendly application integrated within our social 
performance data systems.

In Liberia, this challenge is especially relevant. 
ArcelorMittal Liberia (AML) operates across a wide 
footprint that includes mining areas, transport corridors 
and port facilities. As with many large-scale industrial 
operations in similar contexts, land access and crop 
compensation processes can be complicated, involving 
customary land rights, multiple stakeholders and evolving 
regulatory frameworks. These realities highlighted the 
need for clearer standards, more predictable processes 
and more consistent engagement — creating an 
opportunity to pilot a more structured and transparent 
approach to resettlement and land-related risks.

These improvements were shaped through extensive 
collaboration with local stakeholders. AML held 
consultations with farmers, traditional leaders, community 
representatives, and government authorities across 
Nimba, Grand Bassa, and Bong Counties. Grievance 
mechanisms were expanded so concerns could be raised 
through multiple channels, with oversight from the Mine 
Resettlement Coordination Committee – a multi 
stakeholder forum that includes farmer unions and local 
authorities. This collaborative approach has helped build 
shared understanding and greater confidence in 
land-related decision-making.

Affected communities investigation approach
During the year, we also finalised the Affected Communities 
Investigation Manual and supporting tools to strengthen our 
capability to investigate community-related grievances. 
The manual complements the company’s Grievance 
Procedure by focusing specifically on managing grievances 
raised by affected communities.

It provides clear, practical guidance for investigators, 
including a consistent investigation framework and 
step-by-step instructions covering initial assessment, 
evidence gathering, culturally sensitive interviews, impact 
analysis, documentation of findings, and the development 
of remediation recommendations. A suite of templates, 
checklists, and interview guides supports consistent 
application across operations.

The experience in Liberia is now informing group-wide work 
to harmonise land and resettlement practices across our 
sites, supporting a more consistent, transparent and 
rights-based approach globally.

In response, AML introduced a new Crop Compensation 
and Land Lease Policy, effective July 2025, supported 
by an automated Digital Crop Compensation System. 
Together, they establish a structured seven-step process

identifies priority needs through territorial diagnostics

Education is a core pillar of this strategy, supporting the

Our disciplined approach to needs assessment,

Planet
Climate Action and the Clean Energy Transition

Decarbonising steelmaking – A transition plan  
for ArcelorMittal Dunkirk 
The 10th February 2026 marked an important milestone 
in ArcelorMittal’s decarbonisation journey. Following 
positive European policy developments and the signing of 
a competitive low-carbon electricity2 contract in France, 
we announced a €1.3 billion investment to proceed with 
the construction of a new electric arc furnace (EAF) at our 
Dunkirk site. Replacing a blast furnace (BF) with an EAF is 
a significant commitment and investment, and it is the 
largest ArcelorMittal has made in Europe since the company 
was created in 2006. The 2 million tonne EAF will in due course 
replace BF3, one of the two current blast furnaces, which is 
anticipated to reduce absolute emissions by approximately 
25% at the site. The new EAF has the potential to produce steel 
with a Scope 1-2 intensity of potentially as low as 0.6 tonnes 
CO2e per tonne.

The intention is that ArcelorMittal’s investments in EAFs will 
be sequential – with the next investment expected to be 
announced when Dunkirk is closer to completion. Should the 
policy environment demonstrate further positive momentum 
– including on energy costs and the demand signal – the 
timeline can be amended appropriately.

## Planet – Climate Action and the Clean Energy Transition continued

How the transition unfolds in practice?
ArcelorMittal Dunkirk, with an annual capacity of 5.5 million 
tonnes, currently produces flat steel through two blast 
furnaces, which can use up to 25% recycled steel per tonne  
of steel produced. The site also operates a coke oven, 
a sinter plant, ladle treatment stations, continuous casters

and a hot rolled strip mill. In its current configuration,  
the blast furnace route has an average carbon intensity3  
of between 1.73 and 1.9 tonnes of CO2 per tonne of steel  
(tCO2e/tCS), varying with operating conditions such  
as utilisation rates, which can improve or worsen the  
overall emissions profile.

(see project timeline below). This timeframe reflects  
the need to relocate existing installations to create  
space for the new EAF. Although the investment was formally 
confirmed in February 2026, the site had already been 
preparing the project through successive, structured 
engineering phases designed to progressively refine the 
technical definition and prepare for implementation.

The installation of the EAF will introduce a second, 
complementary steelmaking route, improving both 
production flexibility and economic resilience while 
maintaining total capacity. The larger blast furnace  
(3.5 Mt nameplate capacity) will remain operational, 
while a new 2.0 Mt EAF will produce steel using low-carbon 
electricity as its primary energy input. The EAF will be 
charged with a mix of metallic inputs consisting of around 
60% recycled scrap, 20% Direct Reduced Iron (DRI) and/or 
Hot Briquetted Iron (HBI), which will be sourced externally 
depending on market conditions, and 20% hot metal 
from the remaining blast furnace. This mix has been 
chosen to ensure the new EAF can produce the full range 
of steel products currently manufactured through  
the blast furnace route.

## ArcelorMittal Dunkirk’s transition plan

Following the final investment decision, detailed 
engineering, procurement, construction and  
commissioning are expected to take approximately 
42 months. The anticipated ramp-up period of the new  
EAF is approximately 18-24 months, where production  
and operational performance are progressively increased 
after construction. The smaller 2.0 Mt blast furnace will be 
permanently idled only once the EAF is fully commissioned 
and operating at full capacity. In practice, this is not 
expected to occur before 2030.
