3) Simplified permitting 
The French Industrie Verte framework streamlines 
authorisation procedures for strategic industrial projects  
by running administrative review and public consultation  
in parallel rather than sequentially. This reduces the average 
environmental permitting timeline while maintaining 
requirements on environmental protection, safety  
and transparency.

The Economic Realities of the Clean Energy Transition 
The final investment decision on the Dunkirk EAF is an 
important step forward.

What made the investment decision possible?
The low-carbon transition of hard to abate sectors, in  
most cases, will not occur through a single transformational 
step, but through a sequence of decisions shaped by 
infrastructure, policy and market conditions. In the case  
of Dunkirk, moving from concept to investment required 
several enabling factors to be in place:

It is no secret however, that as far as the steel industry is 
concerned, progress has been slower than initially anticipated. 
Expectations that cheap green hydrogen would arrive 
quickly, or carbon capture would scale smoothly, are now 
understood to have been overestimated, considerably 
delaying industrial transition plans in many sectors.

1) Policy framework 
The transition to EAF steelmaking depends on conditions 
beyond the site itself. Trade protection, carbon pricing  
and emerging markets for lower-emissions steel directly 
influence the business case.

2) Funding support
Public funding support is required to mitigate the significant 
capital expenditure of transitioning to lower-carbon 
technologies. The Dunkirk EAF project is supported by 
Energy Efficiency Certificates (CEE), a regulatory mechanism 
introduced by the French Government in 2005 that promotes 
energy savings and CO2 reduction and will cover 50% of the 
total project cost.

4) Long-term supply of affordable low-carbon electricity
Electrified steelmaking requires stable and competitively 
priced low-carbon power. To secure this, we signed  
a Nuclear Power Production Allocation Contract (CAPN)  
with EDF at the end of last year. The agreement provides 
access to a dedicated share of generation from its 
operating nuclear fleet for 18 years, improving cost visibility 
and enabling reliable operation of the new EAF.

To understand why, it is useful to review the evolution of the 
clean energy transition to date. Its roots took hold in the 
1980s, but it was only in the 2010s that the transition really 
began, when clean energy became an economic option as 
solar and wind costs collapsed due to scale and learning. 
Today renewables can be the cheapest source of new 
electricity (albeit intermittent), and furthermore governments 
increasingly view clean energy as a strategic asset that can 
reduce reliance on imported fuels.

Proposed steel trade measures are expected to reduce 
import pressure and support stable utilisation rates,

5) Industrial collaboration
The site also benefits from its location within Dunkirk’s 
low-carbon industrial cluster Zone Industrielle Bas Carbone 
(ZIBaC), which brings together multiple stakeholders  
to plan shared decarbonisation systems (see Dunkirk’s 
decarbonisation cluster). By developing common regional 
infrastructure rather than site-specific solutions, the 
industrial cluster reduces implementation risk and  
supports the long-term viability of the transition for  
all stakeholders involved.

## CASE STUDY

Building a low-carbon industrial ecosystem –  
Dunkirk’s decarbonisation cluster

The energy transition is not just a fuel switch but an entire 
system transition that is not equal in speed, shape and 
evenness. Without policy support, the economics for 
decarbonising iron- and steelmaking are challenging and, 
as a result, the transition is still in its early stages (see section 
How to decarbonise heavy industry: A spotlight on Europe).

The port of Dunkirk has long been home to heavy industry, 
with ArcelorMittal Dunkirk, other manufacturing companies 
and energy producers at its heart. Such is the significance 
of the industrial cluster, Dunkirk accounts for around  
20% of France’s industrial emissions.

programme that maps out the infrastructure needed to  
cut emissions across the port. Dunkirk’s ZIBaC was the  
first zone to win such a grant, as part of the France 2030 
investment plan.

In fact, a growing number of leading institutions are now 
highlighting the difficulties of being able to limit the global 
average temperature increase to 1.5°C above pre-industrial 
levels and the probability of an overshoot. For example, the 
International Energy Agency (IEA) stated in its World Energy 
Outlook 2025 that “it is now all but certain that 1.5 degrees  
of warming will be exceeded within a decade or less, and 
that pathways that limit this overshoot to low levels have 
now slipped out of reach.”4

Looking beyond 2030, the site also hosts several pilot 
decarbonisation projects, including studies to further 
increase scrap use in the blast furnace and the EU-funded 
3D demonstrator using the DMX® CO2 capture process, 
which captures around 0.5 tonnes of CO2 per hour from 
steelmaking gases, an early step towards a future North Sea 
carbon capture and storage (CCS) network. Together with 
the new EAF, these initiatives portray our phased approach: 
near-term emissions reductions from steelmaking, with 
deeper decarbonisation dependent on the future 
availability of low-carbon ironmaking routes.

The ongoing infrastructure planning, covering electricity, 
hydrogen, CO2 management, water and heat networks, 
is expected to facilitate the wider regional transition to 
lower-carbon industrial processes. Developments at 
cluster level are expected to support the long-term 
feasibility of decarbonisation options under consideration 
for our Dunkirk site by improving access to shared 
infrastructure and reducing individual project risks.

To support decarbonisation, and recognising that 
decarbonising industry requires infrastructure that extends 
beyond the boundary of any single site, Zone Industrielle 
Bas Carbone (ZIBaC) was created to develop shared 
low-carbon systems.

Coordinated by EcosystèmeD, a public organisation  
that supports innovative projects, it brings together over 
460 companies, including ArcelorMittal, and public 
authorities to embark on one of Europe’s most ambitious 
decarbonisation transformations. It aims to turn Dunkirk 
into a major hub for sustainable industry and to align the 
region with France’s 2050 net-zero goals.

This transparency is helpful as it enables an honest 
conversation about the realities of what is achievable 
against the context of a rapidly declining carbon budget.

To accelerate its transformation, in 2024 the French state 
awarded €13.6 million to the Dunkirk cluster’s DKarbonation 
project — a comprehensive engineering and feasibility

4.	 IEA temperature outcomes are scenario-based projections.  
The World Energy Outlook presents multiple scenarios, none  
of which constitutes a forecast or prediction of the future.

## Expected timeline

Assessing our near-term trajectory and long-term ambition
While the well-below 2°C threshold remains an important 
reference point for ambition, recent global assessments, 
including the IEA’s referenced earlier, indicate it is becoming 
increasingly difficult to achieve in practice. Therefore, 
our plans are grounded in what can realistically be 
delivered within existing economic, technological and 
infrastructure constraints.

Reframing our strategic focus
We have reframed the way we are taking strategic  
decisions to both reduce the risks and capture economic 
opportunities from the new value pools the energy transition 
brings. In recognition of the varied maturity and momentum 
across different parts of the transition, we are separating 
our actions into three structural themes: renewable energy; 
materials and solutions; and the transformation of our 
operations. Across these three focus areas, revenues from 
renewables, electrical steels, selected EAF/DRI production, 
and low‑carbon building panels represented 13% of total 
company revenues in 20256.

Investing directly in renewable energy 
While the scale and economics of low-carbon emissions 
energy are not yet sufficient to transition to green-hydrogen 
direct reduced iron (DRI) based steelmaking, we have 
identified the opportunity to invest directly in the new value 
pools created by the expansion of clean power systems.

its annual power capacity is equivalent to powering nearly 
10 million Indian households. This is expected to provide over 
20% of the existing energy requirements to AM/NS India’s Hazira 
plant and to support emissions reductions of 1.5 million tonnes.

Enabling the transition through materials and solutions
If the power sector has passed the tipping point, transport 
and buildings are now approaching their own inflection 
point in terms of adoption of lower-carbon emissions 
technologies. As transport electrification, renewable 
generation, grid expansion and building efficiency all 
require large volumes of high-quality and specialised 
materials, this is also directly impacting ArcelorMittal’s 
strategic decisions and investments.

Looking beyond 2030, the pace of further decarbonisation 
will depend on external factors including energy costs, 
technology maturity, demand growth and policy design.  
Our long-term strategy is designed to remain flexible  
and adaptive, positioning the company to accelerate  
when conditions allow.

We are developing a portfolio of high-quality renewable 
energy assets in regions with good natural resources and 
enabling conditions. This is an exciting, attractive new area 
of business for ArcelorMittal, delivering a guaranteed rate  
of return with an average Internal Rate Return (IRR) over 12%. 
These renewable assets have a guaranteed offtake demand 
with our steel sites, and the steel facility benefits from access 
to reliable green renewable energy at an attractive price.

With additional projects in Brazil (819MW) and Argentina 
(130MW), we now have 1.9GW of renewable capacity 
commissioned (1.6GW on an equity share basis). Combined, 
this is expected to deliver EBITDA of US$200 million.

In the longer term, we maintain our ambition to achieve 
net-zero greenhouse gas emissions by 2050. This covers our 
Scope 1 and Scope 2 emissions, as well as those Scope 3 
emissions (see chapter 10, Advancing supplier data quality 
for Scope 3 transparency) over which we have significant 
ability to influence outcomes across our value chain5.

Given the success of these initial projects, a further 1.4GW is 
now under development and should be commissioned by 
2028 (1.2GW on a equity share basis). This includes 200MW in 
Brazil, 180MW in Argentina, and a further 1GW across three 
projects in India (Maharashtra, Rajasthan and Gujarat)7.

Increasingly, we are directing investment towards the 
production of innovative products that are closely linked  
to the expansion of low-carbon energy systems and 
efficient infrastructure.

On 8th May 2025, we announced that ArcelorMittal’s first and 
largest renewable energy venture had started providing clean 
electricity to AM/NS India. Developed as a hybrid project, it 
integrates 1GW of solar and wind capacity with a third-party 
hydro pumped storage solution, to deliver at least 250MW of 
round-the-clock power. Vast in scale the solar site is spread 
over 2,400 acres, equivalent to more than 1,700 FIFA size 
football fields and with the wind site spread over 700 acres. 
Now fully commissioned and running at nameplate capacity,

Electrical steels are critical to both the low-carbon and 
digital transitions, supporting electrified transport, 
renewable energy generation, power grids and data 
infrastructure. As demand for these applications grows, 
so does the need for high-performance electrical steels. 
We are therefore tripling our annual electrical steel 
production capacity through investment in a new electrical 
steel production line in Mardyck, with an expected $100mn 
additional EBITDA benefit, adding to our existing European 
electrical steels capacity in Saint-Chély d’Apcher.

These projects are also expected to generate EBITDA  
of US$200 million.

5.	 Certain Scope 3 emissions, including those from joint ventures

the pace of decarbonisation in those activities, which is ultimately 
driven by local enabling conditions. Nevertheless, we continue to 
engage with partners, customers and other stakeholders across 
our value chain to support and encourage faster emissions 
reductions wherever feasible.

The total investment amount of all renewable projects  
is approximately US$2 billion.

## Planet – Climate Action and the Clean Energy Transition continued

We expect to have 3.4 Mt of additional EAF steelmaking 
capacity in operation by the end of 2026, bringing total 
group EAF capacity to approximately 27.4 Mt. This  
capacity comes from two projects in Spain (Gijon and 
Sestao) and the new EAF in the US (Calvert), which has 
commissioned and is ramping up production. When the 
recently announced EAF in Dunkirk, France (see A transition 
plan for ArcelorMittal Dunkirk) is completed, additional  
EAF capacity is expected to increase to 5.4 million tonnes  
– representing an additional 22.5% when compared with  
current EAF capacity (24Mt). This new aggregated EAF 
capacity is estimated to come online at an average ~0.7 
tCO2e/t CS, ~60% below the current group carbon emissions. 
However, all sites have the potential to reduce emissions 
further dependent on the metallic inputs.

We are investing in upgrades across our global operations: 
expanding DR-pellet production at our Port Cartier plant in 
Canada from 3 million tonnes to 10 million tonnes; boosting 
DR-pellet feed at the Las Truchas mine in Mexico with an 
additional 0.3 million tonnes capacity, and increasing DR 
quality pellet feed production in Brazil to 4.5 million tonnes 
per year. These strategic investments position us strongly to 
scale up DRI production when the business case demands.

We have also announced a new electrical steels 
manufacturing facility in Calvert, Alabama, designed to 
supply automotive, renewable energy and industrial motor 
applications. The plant is expected to have a capacity of 
up to 150,000 tonnes per year. Construction started in the 
second half of 2025 with start-up expected in 2027. The 
project is expected to have $200mn additional EBITDA 
benefit and to create up to 1,300 jobs during construction 
and more than 200 permanent roles once operational.

a single, ready-to-install module. This system weighs  
around half as much as traditional roofing with separate 
solar panels and has a carbon footprint around 25% lower 
than conventional insulated steel roofs, while delivering  
high-energy performance (See Helioroof® case study in 
chapter 6).

Together, these initiatives demonstrate how our products 
are central to the supply chain of the energy transition.

We also continue exploring alternative carbon sources  
to replace coal. At Juiz de Fora (Brazil) we already use 
sustainably produced biochar in blast furnaces, leading  
to production emissions lower than 100 kg of CO2e per tonne 
of steel.  ArcelorMittal Tubarão is currently evaluating  
a business case, in collaboration with a Brazilian partner,  
to utilize sugarcane bagasse, an agricultural by-product 
from sugar production, for the generation of biocarbon 
intended to supply our operations. This initiative aims 
to enhance circularity within the value chain and offers 
significant potential for CO2 emissions reduction, as the 
resulting biocarbon would be used in our blast furnaces 
as a substitute for injected pulverized coal (PCI).

In the built environment, the transition requires materials  
and design approaches that reduce embodied carbon, 
improve thermal performance and support the integration 
of renewable energy. We are providing not only products 
but also systems, expertise and innovation across the 
building life cycle to support more sustainable, future-ready 
construction. Our approach includes holistic services like 
Steligence®, which promotes design that uses materials 
more efficiently, reduces environmental impact and unlocks 
value across the life cycle of buildings and infrastructure.  
By integrating high-performance steel solutions with design 
thinking, we help architects, developers and engineers 
optimise structures for durability, energy efficiency and 
lower whole-life carbon footprints.

Transforming our own operations
Since 2018, we have reduced our absolute Scope 1 and 2 
emissions by 27% on an adjusted basis8. On a historical basis, 
which includes emissions from assets that have since been 
closed or divested, total Scope 1 and 2 emissions have 
decreased by 47.7% over the same period. To put this into 
perspective, since 2018, assets divested and decommissioned 
have typically been of a higher average carbon intensity 
(~2.5 tCO2e/tCS) contributing to a group carbon intensity in 
2025 of 1.79 tCO2e/tCS.

The company is also working on modernising the  
existing EAF capacity. The SteelUp! programme at Belval  
in Luxembourg will replace the 1997 EAF with a more 
automated and digitally controlled unit, with commissioning 
expected in 2026. The new EAF is expected to reduce energy 
consumption by around 15% and lower CO2 emissions by 
approximately 200,000 tonnes per year, bringing the 
carbon intensity of finished steel to below 300 kg CO2 per 
tonne. The project also includes raw material optimisation 
enabled by using hot briquetted iron (HBI). These upgrades 
expand the site’s ability to produce higher-grade steels 
using electric steelmaking routes and improve overall 
operational efficiency.

Further emissions reductions will come from strategic 
projects across our assets. Their sequencing will reflect 
economic and technical readiness: solutions that are 
already viable are implemented first, while more capital-
intensive or less mature pathways are phased in as costs, 
infrastructure and policy conditions evolve.

3. Building readiness for breakthrough technologies
While increasing our percentage of EAF capacity and 
production helps reduce carbon emissions, it must be 
remembered that ultimately iron-making must also 
decarbonise for the steel industry to achieve near-zero. 
Therefore, we continue to invest in the development and 
testing of technologies that are expected to play a critical 
role in the long-term decarbonisation of iron and steelmaking. 
These include hydrogen utilisation, carbon capture 
utilisation and storage, and electrolysis-based ironmaking. 
While these technologies are far from being cost-competitive 
at scale, they represent the main pathways for achieving 
deep reductions in ironmaking emissions in the future. Their 
deployment will depend on the availability of large volumes 
of competitively priced low-carbon electricity, making the 
future of ironmaking closely linked to the evolution of the 
energy system described earlier.

One example is our insulated steel solutions,  
which are designed to deliver high thermal efficiency  
while maintaining structural strength and low weight.  
Their performance and ease of installation make sandwich  
panels a cost-effective option for roofing and façades, 
helping customers to reduce operational energy use.  
In 2025, we inaugurated the Helioroof® production line  
in Contrisson, France, a new roofing production line that 
integrates steel panels, insulation and solar cells into

1. Transitioning steelmaking assets to EAF 
With low-carbon ironmaking not yet cost-competitive,  
we are pursuing a phased approach starting with 
steelmaking, where in certain markets and under certain 
scenarios electric arc furnaces represent a viable option. 
Today, EAF capacity represents 26% of our total production, 
up from 19% in 2018.

2. Securing high-grade iron and metallics
For secondary steel production, securing high-quality scrap 
is critical. Over the past few years, we have acquired three 
additional recycling businesses in Europe with a combined 
processing capacity of nearly 1 million tonnes of scrap steel. 
And in Sestao, we are implementing an artificial intelligence 
(AI) model to improve the segmentation of different 
materials within ferrous scrap. This innovation will enhance 
sorting precision and support the production of high-quality 
steel from recycled inputs.

Building state-of-the-art EAFs allows us to produce steel 
with significantly lower direct emissions by increasing the 
use of scrap and other lower-carbon metallic inputs, such  
as DRI and hot briquetted iron (HBI), which can be produced 
using natural gas instead of coal.

Similarly, a reliable supply of affordable, high-quality 
metallic inputs is essential for decarbonising primary  
steel production. Securing these materials is critical to 
scaling up DRI production and accelerating the transition  
to DRI–EAF steelmaking.

## Planet – Climate Action and the Clean Energy Transition continued

Pivotal policy developments
In October 2025, the Commission proposed sharply reduced 
steel Tariff Rate Quotas (TRQs) which will limit cheap imports 
and reduce global overcapacity entering the EU market. This 
is expected to improve the capacity utilization of domestic 
steel mills. A political agreement on the proposal was 
reached between the European Parliament, the Commission 
and the Council in April, with the measure now proceeding 
through formal adoption to take effect from 1 July 2026.

Emissions Trading System 
Also tabled for this year is a review of the Emissions Trading 
System (ETS), the cornerstone of the EU’s policy to combat 
climate change. With the EU recently actively confronting 
the challenges its industry faces to remain competitive at a 
global level, including high energy costs, while at the same 
time confirming its 2040 climate target, there is a live debate 
on the future shape of the ETS. Some member states are even 
calling for the whole system to be abolished, and while this 
seems unlikely, some level of revision is now to be expected 
given the public recognition that changes are required to 
help industry meet climate goals, also reinforced at the 
trialogue agreement around the 2040 climate target.

While steel is recognised as a strategic sector, which is 
positive, it is not currently included in the products with a 
“Made in Europe” requirement. Instead it is solely assessed 
on the basis of low-carbon criteria. In this respect, it was also 
expected that the proposal would include a framework for 
defining low-carbon products, supporting labelling or 
certification. This would help create a strong demand signal 
for European low-carbon materials, including green steel. 
However, as in the published proposal the label will be 
defined by delegated acts under a different legal 
framework, which brings uncertainty both in parameters and 
in the timing. We see this as a missed opportunity and will 
continue to press the case for steel’s inclusion in this list of 
“Made in Europe” products and for the faster development 
of a dedicated label for low-carbon steel.

We are therefore pursuing a structured programme of  
pilots, demonstrations and partnerships to build technical, 
operational and commercial readiness ahead of large-
scale deployment. The ArcelorMittal XCarb® Innovation Fund 
continues to invest in companies developing technologies 
with strong potential to decarbonise our operations.  
Our efforts to capture carbon using MHI Carbon Capture 
technology are showing promising results, although the 
technology remains at an early stage of development. 
And while green hydrogen is still far from being economically 
viable as a reductant in DRI production, it is already being 
deployed in certain finishing processes to replace 
fossil-based fuels.

This was followed by the Carbon Border Adjustment Mechanism 
(CBAM) that came into force on 1st January 2026, moving from 
a transitional reporting exercise into an operational carbon 
pricing mechanism that affects real trade and costs.

This portfolio approach reduces the risk of future 
investments, strengthens our understanding of performance 
and cost drivers, and maintains our strategic flexibility  
as enabling conditions evolve, allowing us to move rapidly 
once they become economically viable.

A fully functioning CBAM is a crucial step forward although 
data on emissions reductions and trade shifts will only be 
available post-2027 reporting. We continue to champion 
further changes to expand the scope, close loopholes, 
strengthen anti-circumvention measures and avoid 
resource shuffling.

How to decarbonise heavy industry: 
A spotlight on Europe
Matching ambition with reality: The structural  
challenges of decarbonising heavy industry
Europe is one of the most committed continents when  
it comes to actively shaping policy that supports an 
accelerated energy transition. However, as a first mover, 
Europe’s energy transition has encountered economic, 
political, technical and geopolitical challenges.

Together, these developments are crucial and mean  
that steelmakers start 2026 with a new level of confidence 
about the long-term health of the industry. We are very 
appreciative of the time that European leaders have 
dedicated to developing policy that supports the industry.

Looking ahead, we are hopeful that other policies in the 
pipeline will further strengthen the investment case for  
low-carbon steelmaking in Europe.

While the EU’s ETS and supportive policy worked relatively 
well for power generation, it has been demonstrably less 
effective for industries, including steel, where emissions are 
process-related, alternatives are capital intensive and not 
mature, and carbon leakage is real.

Industrial Accelerator Act
The Industrial Accelerator Act is an EU policy initiative 
designed to accelerate investment and strengthen Europe’s 
industrial competitiveness, and complement the Net-Zero 
Industry Act (mainly focusing on net-zero technologies). The 
first proposal of the act was published by the EU Commission 
on 4th March 2026. A central feature is the introduction of 
“Made in Europe” content requirements for public funding and 
public procurement, which would require publicly financed 
purchases of strategic materials and key clean-tech 
equipment – such as electric vehicles, batteries, wind 
components, solar equipment, head pumps and power cables 
– to include a minimum share of EU-manufactured content.

ArcelorMittal, primarily through Eurofer, the European Steel 
Association, has engaged intensively with the European 
Commission and Member States to advocate for the policy 
needed for the European steel industry to decarbonise while 
remaining globally competitive and able to thrive for the 
long-term.

## Planet – Climate Action and the Clean Energy Transition continued

It was previously envisaged that climate transition 
leadership would have three stages of adoption. In  
the first, one region (most probably Europe) or a group of 
regions, would adopt ambitious climate policies, ahead  
of others, to set regulatory norms and stimulate innovation. 
The first movers would bear higher initial costs, but would 
also gain advantages including technology leadership, 
green industry development and regulatory  
influence globally.

Two distinct but related issues are apparently under 
discussion in advance of the upcoming review. First, the 
phase-out of free allowances, currently scheduled to be 
completed by 2034, alongside the CBAM transition. 
Second, the ETS cap itself, which under current legislation is 
set to reach zero by 2039, meaning no allowances would 
remain, whether free or purchased.

## Relative cost of steel

Timing of economic 
tipping points driven

## mainly by policy

ArcelorMittal is supportive of these trajectories being 
revisited, as even with the strengthened TRQ and CBAM, 
it will be difficult for the industry to transition at scale within 
this timeframe. We will engage actively with Member States, 
the European Parliament, and the Commission to provide 
our view on how the ETS can be reformed and made fit 
for purpose.

## Cost of steel

In the second stage, global scaling would occur, with 
technologies and policy frameworks spreading to other 
countries. At this stage the transition shifts from policy 
experimentation to global deployment.

## Cost gap

Only after this would there be system normalisation in  
a third stage, with costs passed through to consumers  
and the broader economy. At this point decarbonisation 
becomes structurally embedded in markets, rather than 
driven primarily by policy mandates.

Energy prices 
These policies do not however address the high electricity 
price that European producers face. Replacing a blast 
furnace with an electric arc furnace doubles the amount of 
electricity required. This becomes far greater again when 
considering H2-DRI+ EAF as Hydrogen steel needs electricity 
both to make hydrogen and to run the electric arc furnace. 
That is why initiatives such as the grids package, the use of 
contracts-for-difference (CfDs) in combination with power 
purchase agreements (PPAs), and the potential creation of 
an Energy Union/seamless single market for energy are vital.

Today
Near-term
Long-term

Green steel cost
	 Conventional steel cost

This has not translated into reality. Few countries have 
followed Europe’s lead and in fact even Europe, which has 
been the stalwart of ambitious climate policy, is now facing 
political pushback from some member states with calls  
to soften elements of the system to protect against 
deindustrialisation.

Policy has a critical role in bringing these tipping points 
closer, provided it is consistent, long-term in orientation, 
and provides incentives sufficient to justify the scale of 
investment required.

In the longer term, as and when enabling conditions 
converge more broadly, lower-carbon production routes 
could become economically competitive with traditional 
BF-BOF pathways, making them the default investment 
choice at a global level. By 2050, we would certainly expect 
there to be more low-emissions iron and steelmaking than 
today. How much more will have a strong correlation with the 
economics and policies to support decarbonisation vectors 
for steel, namely the price of electricity, the cost of CCS, 
the cost of bio-alternatives and CO2 prices regionally or 
globally. A lot depends on what is politically acceptable. 
The cost of low-emission routes will be higher cost than 
those that don’t abate CO2, unless there is a structural 
change, eg ultra-low round-the-clock electricity prices.

Navigating uncertainty post-2030
The emissions-intensity outcome we now expect to achieve 
by 2030 reflects what can be delivered under current market, 
policy and investment conditions, based on projects that 
have reached or are expected to reach final investment 
decision. It therefore represents a realistic pathway rather 
than an aspirational or policy-dependent target.

Concerns around competitiveness, regarding 
industrialisation, inflation and energy security have moved 
to the centre of the debate, leading to increasing scrutiny 
of the EU ETS, not just as a climate tool, but also as an 
economic policy tool. How Europe navigates it will be crucial. 
See: How to decarbonise heavy industry: A spotlight 
on Europe.

Realistically, given where we stand today, conditions for 
the economic decarbonisation of iron-making (crucial to 
achieve net-zero) are likely to remain challenging in the 
next decade. Green hydrogen costs are still too high for 
large-scale DRI transformation, affordable renewable 
electricity supply is uneven, CCUS is limited to a few 
emerging clusters, and breakthrough technologies remain 
at demonstration or pilot scale. By way of an example, green 
hydrogen-based DRI production will become viable in 
Europe when electricity costs approach $30/MWh on 
a continuous basis (with prevailing CO2 prices), a threshold 
that remains out of reach in most markets today.

What does all this mean for the steel industry’s transition? 
Deep decarbonisation of steel is achievable – and we  
are maintaining our ambition of achieving net zero by 2050. 
However, it requires certain tipping points to be reached, 
grounded in economics and thermodynamics, not purely 
in aspiration or targets.

We understand that our stakeholders want clarity on the 
shape and pace of our transition beyond 2030. The last 
decade has shown us, however, that it is very difficult 
to predict with any accuracy what level of emissions 
reductions is achievable given so much uncertainty. 
Deep decarbonisation in steel remains significantly more 
expensive than conventional production, and our transition 
is ultimately dependent on external conditions largely 
beyond our direct control.

Once tipping points are reached, the footprint of the steel 
industry can change quite rapidly – in line with how other 
technology transitions have occurred. We will continue 
to champion the policies that will help accelerate this 
transition, while actively investing in areas of the energy 
transition that support ArcelorMittal’s overall sustainability.

Measuring our progress
1. Operational emissions
We track our carbon performance using a combination  
of operational and emissions indicators, including both 
absolute and intensity-based metrics that reflect the  
scale of our operations and the carbon efficiency of our 
steel production.

Similarly, the production of intermediary products such as 
pig iron increases emissions without increasing the crude 
steel denominator, affecting the intensity calculation. 
Year-on-year changes in intensity therefore reflect both 
decarbonisation efforts and variations in utilisation rates, 
product mix and asset configuration.

only a limited portion of our value chain.

To provide transparency over long-term performance,  
we also report 2018 data adjusted for structural portfolio 
changes, enabling a like-for-like comparison. On this 
basis, the adjusted CO2e intensity (steel and mining) has 
decreased by 4.1% since 2018, from 1.86 tCO2e/tcs to 1.79 
tCO2e/tcs. This reduction shows progress in a sector where 
most producers saw little change in intensity, according to 
external benchmarking conducted for the period 2021-2024 
(SteelWatch 2026). Adjusted absolute emissions (Scope 1 
and 2, steel and mining) have fallen by 27% over the 
same period.

In 2025, absolute CO2e footprint (steel and mining) 
decreased by 3.1% although the adjusted group CO2e 
intensity KPI increased by 1.2% compared with 2024. 
Our steel intensity (Scope 1+2+limited Scope 3, steel only) 
of 1.86 tCO2/tcs in 2025, remained below the worldsteel 
average of 1.92 tCO2/tCS (2024).

Methodological enhancements include the use of 
cradle-to-gate emission factors for a broader range 
of purchased raw materials, including coal (included in 
Category 3); the inclusion of additional downstream 
activities such as emissions from the processing of mining, 
steel and by-products (Category 10 and 11), the 
reclassification of emissions from off-gases sold to third 
parties for heat or power generation from Scope 1 to  
Scope 3 (Category 11) and our equity share of Scope 1 and 2 
emissions from financially material joint ventures, including 
AMNS India (Category 15). Material categories are defined 
using a 5% threshold of total Scope 3 emissions, with  
an additional safeguard ensuring that non-material 
categories do not collectively exceed 20%.

11.	 A significant share of the emissions in this category derives

currently also captured under Scope 1–2 emissions, double 
counting arises from differences in the accounting 
methodologies applied.

Given the ongoing expansion of our mining operations  
and certain joint ventures, we expect emissions in 
categories 10 and 15 to increase in absolute terms over the 
coming years, while continuing to evaluate opportunities 
to improve emissions efficiency across the value chain.

The CO2e intensity metric is sensitive to operational 
configuration. In blast furnace operations, running below 
nominal capacity can reduce process efficiency because

## Planet – Climate Action and the Clean Energy Transition continued

Operating in a changing climate
Regardless of the pace of the transition, climate change  
is already impacting the physical operating environment 
for many industrial sectors. Rising temperatures, changing 
precipitation patterns and more frequent extreme weather 
events have the potential to disrupt operations, damage 
infrastructure and affect workforce safety.

## BOX OUT

ArcelorMittal’s XCarb® Innovation fund advancing 
low-carbon steelmaking through strategic investments
Since 2021, the XCarb® Innovation Fund has invested 
approximately $200 million in nine companies developing 
disruptive technologies with the potential to support the 
decarbonisation of steelmaking. These investments include 
renewable and nuclear energy, energy storage, carbon 
capture and utilisation, electrolysis and biochar production.

Alongside the investment, XCarb® signed a Memorandum 
of Understanding to explore technology validation tests at 
ArcelorMittal’s GasLab facility in Asturias and potential pilot 
and deployment pathways across ArcelorMittal operations. 
This reflects the Fund’s focus not only on investing in 
innovative technologies, but also on accelerating their 
commercialisation and integration into steelmaking.

The screening establishes a basis for integrating  
physical climate considerations into asset planning  
and investment decisions.

Over the past two years, we have carried out a systematic 
assessment of our assets’ exposure to acute and chronic 
climate hazards, including heat, flooding, drought, wildfire, 
wind and extreme precipitation. This screening was 
conducted using a third-party climate data analytics 
platform based on high-resolution, science-based climate 
models that estimate the magnitude, duration and 
likelihood of climate hazards under different climate 
pathways. These global models provide a consistent basis 
for comparing exposure across sites, but do not capture 
site-specific factors such as local defences, topography  
or operational controls that can influence actual risk.

In 2025, we developed a climate adaptation toolkit  
to support detailed, site-specific assessments tailored  
to local conditions, operational characteristics, growth 
plans and environmental management requirements.  
The toolkit includes a climate risk assessment module  
to identify material risks at the process level, together  
with an adaptation planning workbook to guide the design 
of mitigation measures, implementation planning, and 
ongoing monitoring and evaluation. It was piloted at one 
site and is being rolled out across the main steelmaking 
and mining operations, supported by training activities 
to ensure effective adoption.

The Fund’s most recent investment was in Electrified 
Thermal Solutions, a US‑based company that has 
developed a breakthrough technology to electrify 
industrial heat generation, which is one of the key 
challenges in decarbonising heavy industry. Its system

The Fund continues to seek investments in scalable, 
economically viable technologies capable of 
decarbonising the steelmaking value chain.

The screening highlighted three hazards of particular 
relevance:

## –Heat stress in tropical and subtropical regions, including

Mexico, Brazil, Liberia, India, and in parts of Europe, with 
implications for workforce safety and process continuity. 
	
–Flood risk, driven by extreme precipitation and proximity

to rivers or coastal zones, is particularly concentrated in 
Northern Europe, with additional exposure at sites in 
Mexico, Brazil, India and other parts of Europe.

Investment oversight
Oversight of climate-related topics sits within the  
existing governance structure. The Board Sustainability 
Committee reviews strategy and progress, while 
implementation remains the responsibility of local CEOs in 
line with operational accountability.

Major investment decisions are assessed through  
the Investment Allocations Committee (IAC), which 
evaluates both financial returns and consistency with  
the company’s decarbonisation objectives. This ensures 
that economic performance and emissions reduction  
are considered together when allocating capital and 
managing transition risks.

Driving innovation  
for sustainability

Our innovation capacity rests on a broad technical base: 
14 R&D sites in nine countries, employing 1,639 people. 
In 2025, we invested US$335 million in R&D (compared with 
US$285 million in 2024). Our R&D strategy spans sustainable 
product development, e-mobility, clean energy applications, 
decarbonisation, environmental performance and AI.  
In 2025, we launched 23 new products and solutions to 
accelerate sustainable lifestyles (including in automotive) 
and 15 to support sustainable construction, infrastructure 
and energy generation.

Its exoskeleton is made from XCarb® RRP steel produced 
at ArcelorMittal Differdange and Belval from 100% recycled 
scrap and 100% renewable electricity, then fabricated at 
the Steligence® centre. It demonstrates how circularity 
and low-carbon steel can transform performance and 
footprint at project scale: 333 kg CO2/t beams, 10.4 t CO2 
saved per beam of 4.8 t and a structure seven to ten times 
lighter than reinforced concrete, thus cutting material use 
and transport emissions. The project targets the highest 
available certification standards and shows what becomes 
possible when materials and design work in harmony.

2030 (McKinsey & Company, Unlocking the European 
AI revolution | McKinsey and The future of US hyperscale 
data centres | McKinsey), we strengthened our offering 
across nearly all core components – from structural steels 
and insulated panels to steel fibres for high load flooring, 
components for racks and backup power, and integrated 
PV insulated roofs such as Helioroof®. Through XCarb® RRP 
steels, we helped customers cut embodied carbon by up 
to 65%. These capabilities allow us to support data centre 
developers under intensifying pressure to build faster, 
operate more efficiently and reduce environmental impact.

The world is shifting fast – technologically, environmentally 
and economically. Our customers are re-engineering their 
business models to meet climate commitments, electrify 
fleets, build digital infrastructure at scale, and deliver 
materials that can stand up to more volatile weather. 
Regulators, investors and communities are asking for 
greater transparency, lower emissions and more resilient 
operations. Inside our operations, expectations for safety 
are high, our systems are increasingly real time and digital, 
production routes are evolving, and our people 
are upskilling.

We organised our work around three priorities that shape 
how we deliver impact across the business:

In construction, the push toward integrated, lower 
carbon systems is reshaping the role of steel in buildings. 
Helioroof®, launched in October 2025, integrates steel 
roofing, insulation and photovoltaic modules into one 
low-carbon system – generating up to 2.1 kWp per module 
and reducing installation complexity, embodied carbon 
and overall project timelines. It represents the next 
evolution of steel’s role in construction: from passive 
material to active energy infrastructure.

In energy, rapid growth in renewable power and grid 
expansion continues to raise performance expectations for 
steels used in harsh or high load environments. We continued 
expanding materials for renewable energy applications. 
Hyper high strength steels (up to 700 MPa) and Magnelis® 
coatings (up to 800 g/m2) were prepared for customer 
testing after development cycles reduced by 60% through 
digital modelling. XCarb® RRP Heavy Plates were again 
selected by Vestas for the Nordlicht 1 offshore wind project. 
We advanced HyMatch® for hydrogen pipelines and 
supplied high-performance non-oriented steels (NOES) to 
Siemens Energy. Our SmartSheetPile technology, integrating 
sensors and AI, expanded its potential to support climate-
resilient infrastructure. These solutions help customers 
accelerate renewable energy deployment while managing 
cost, durability and emissions.

1.	  Supporting customers through the transition to a 
low-carbon and circular economy in digital infrastructure, 
low-carbon construction, clean energy and e-mobility.
2.	  Advancing the technologies that reduce emissions and 
modernise our production routes, from near-term 
improvements to breakthrough pathways.
3.	  Scaling digital and AI enabled performance to transform 
safety, reliability, quality and environmental monitoring 
– supported by stronger governance and capability.

Innovation does not exist in a silo. It connects materials 
development, decarbonisation, digital transformation, 
advanced automation and workforce capability. In 2025, 
we focused on the areas where innovation would have 
the greatest impact on safety, quality, competitiveness 
and long-term resilience.

Digitalisation is also creating entirely new markets with their 
own material and performance requirements. Data centres 
are becoming one of the defining industrial assets of the 
next decade, driven by AI, cloud computing and streaming.

1. Supporting our customers through the energy transition
As these operational capabilities strengthened, we also 
saw our customers continuing their own transitions across 
construction, energy, mobility and digital infrastructure. 
Innovation in 2025 focused on helping them manage these 
transitions without compromising on performance, cost 
or resilience. Across construction, data centres, renewable 
energy and mobility, we accelerated solutions that reflect 
where demand is growing most quickly and where steel must 
evolve to remain the material of choice. These shifts are 
reshaping demand across all our key customer segments.

## “ While all areas are important, a company is only as good

as the products or services it sells. And we are constantly 
challenging and re-inventing what is possible with steel.

ArcelorMittal from our peers, create a competitive 
advantage for our company through innovation and 
ensure that we are true to our purpose and really do 
deliver smarter steels for people and planet.”
	 – Jean-Luc Thirion, Head of R&D

The demand for lower emissions buildings is driving 
adoption of new structural materials and design 
approaches. Our XCarb® recycled and renewably produced 
(RRP) portfolio has become central to sustainable building 
design. Our Kirchberg headquarters in Luxembourg, is rising 
quickly and puts our Steligence® principles into practice, 
using 95% XCarb® RRP steel.

## Planet – Driving innovation for sustainability continued

2. Advancing emissions-reduction technologies
Decarbonising the steel value chain is one of the most 
complex industrial challenges in the world. The technologies 
required span decades, and the transition must be 
balanced against the realities of cost, energy availability, 
regional constraints and the long-lived nature of steel 
assets. In 2025, our approach to innovation in GHG emissions 
reduction was to continue to make progress on all fronts 
because no single pathway will deliver the full transition on 
its own. Our innovation focused on strengthening EAF routes, 
improving performance in existing blast furnace-basic 
oxygen furnace (BF-BOF) assets and advancing breakthrough 
technologies such as low-temperature electrolysis.

In 2025, we moved from standalone tools to integrated 
systems where AI supports enhanced safety performance, 
improves uptime predictability, manages product quality 
and strengthens operational resilience.

Mobility is experiencing its own transformation as 
electrification reshapes how vehicles are designed and 
manufactured. In 2025, we advanced our S in motion® 
solutions for battery electric vehicles (BEVs) with Usibor® 
2000 for lighter, safer structures; scaled advanced 
high-strength steel (AHSS) options for battery and occupant 
protection; and expanded our Multi-Part Integration (MPI) 
solution – which simplifies the vehicle manufacturing 
process by consolidating multiple parts into one – reducing 
original equipment manufacturers (OEM) labour by up to 30% 
and improving precision for automated lines. Our iCARe® 
electrical steel portfolio also progressed further, including 
commercialised 420Save 2714 for EV powertrains. These 
developments support OEMs in redesigning vehicles for 
efficiency, safety and manufacturability.

Trials at Contrecoeur, Canada and Acindar, Argentina 
demonstrated promising reductions in energy use 
compared with conventional pelletising methods. Together, 
these developments strengthen both the economics and 
the environmental performance of EAF routes.

## “ AI is not a standalone programme – it is embedded

in our Digital Excellence Strategy. It acts as a capability  
layer across our digital core, helping us run systems  
more efficiently, optimise business processes and  
create new value that improves safety and  
differentiates our operations.”
	 – Nik Puri, Group CIO

At the same time, blast-furnace-based assets remain 
central to our footprint, making performance improvements 
vital. For plants expected to remain in operation for years 
ahead, reducing emissions at source remains essential.  
We continued feasibility assessments for green oxy-blast 
furnace top-gas recycling, which creates a more energy- 
rich furnace atmosphere and reduces reliance on coke.  
Across reheating facilities, digital furnace-efficiency models 
were industrialised at 23 furnaces in 19 plants, improving 
energy use and raising output by up to 7%. A new internal  
gas-management optimisation model further reduced 
dependence on natural gas by increasing the use of process 
gases. These interventions make existing routes more 
efficient and lower carbon while new pathways mature.

The safety and well-being of our people is a key area where 
this transformation can help us to detect risks earlier and 
intervene faster. In 2025, we developed new AI-enabled 
approaches to support our continuing work on safety 
performance. The following examples illustrate some of the 
risks AI now helps us address – from site-wide hazards to 
highly specific, task-related exposures. At Tubarão, Brazil,  
our iRedZone approach is used to analyse live CCTV feeds 
and identify workers entering hazardous zones, enabling 
faster alerts and interventions than human monitoring alone. 
iMoveSafe, a new fatigue monitoring system was 
implemented in Vega, Brazil, strengthening protection  
in safety critical roles. In Dofasco, Canada, AI-trained fire 
recognition models provide earlier detection of smoke 
or flames. These capabilities extend across a wide range 
of operational scenarios, from broad area monitoring 
to specific, task-related risk profiles. Platforms such as 
SafetyHub, in Acindar, Argentina and SafetyTower in  
Brazil have been integrating multiple monitoring systems 
into unified dashboards, giving teams a clearer and faster 
picture of emerging risk. Together, these developments 
signal a shift from pure physical supervision-based safety  
to also harnessing intelligence-based prevention. Keeping 
processes stable requires systems that anticipate issues 
rather than only react to them, especially in continuous 
production environments. Our predictive maintenance 
platform Sentinel is reshaping how we manage reliability. 
After successful pilots in Canada and France,  
it moved online and delivered exceptional early 
performance. Production lines where Sentinel is deployed 
reported reduced unplanned issues. The shift from reactive 
intervention to predictive insight improves uptime, reduces 
energy waste and strengthens operational continuity across 
our sites.

Strengthening our EAF routes is a key near-term priority. 
In 2025 we piloted AI-enabled scrap optimisation systems 
at our plants in Differdange, Luxembourg and Sestao, 
Spain, generating deeper insight into scrap yield and 
furnace behaviour. At Piracicaba, Brazil, vision-AI and 
machine-learning scrap quality models helped predict how 
copper, tin, chromium and nickel combinations influence 
final properties, providing the basis for more stable and 
predictable EAF production. Cold agglomeration offers 
a promising low-energy alternative to traditional iron 
pelletising, supporting future DRI and EAF pathways.

Together these developments strengthen steel’s role 
as a lower-carbon, high-performance material foundation 
for the next generation of buildings, infrastructure, energy 
systems and electric vehicles.

Progress across EAF routes, BF BOF optimisation and 
breakthrough technologies reflects the realities of our 
footprint: different geographies, energy systems and 
production routes require different solutions. Advancing  
all pathways in parallel strengthens our competitiveness 
today and our readiness for tomorrow’s technologies.

Further details can be found in chapter 5.

## Planet – Driving innovation for sustainability continued

AI and digital systems are embedded in the way we protect 
people, maintain equipment, stabilise quality and monitor 
environmental performance helping create safer, more 
consistent and more reliable operations.

advanced materials for mobility, hydrogen, wind and grid; 
and diversified decarbonisation pathways – from EAF 
optimisation and digital furnace models to breakthrough 
technologies. The focus is on optionality and discipline: 
validating what works at industrial scale, scaling it where  
it delivers value and investing in the governance and skills 
that turn initiatives into enduring capability. By reinforcing 
the capabilities that connect our products, operations, 
technologies and people, we are building an innovation 
system designed for resilience in a decade defined by 
rapid, interdependent change.

Sustaining this momentum requires a stronger digital 
architecture behind the scenes. Effective AI requires  
a modern IT ecosystem. We continued building a layered 
approach with strategic partners: cloud infrastructure,  
a data mesh strategy within each segment and a maturing 
IT operating model. Partners provide out of the box 
platforms and pre-packaged algorithms we can tune to  
our needs, and they support skills transfer so teams can 
operate the latest technology – our Cloud-First, Data-
Centric, Team-Enabled approach. Most plant level AI 
systems operate inside our network, ensuring resilience 
if cloud providers experience issues.

Circularity also places new demands on quality management. 
As scrap becomes more integrated into more grades of 
steelmaking, managing scrap quality becomes a challenge 
of data as much as metallurgy. In 2025, AI helped us manage 
variability in scrap quality across the full scrap life cycle – 
procurement, impurity assessment and modelling of how 
different residual metals influence final properties. A digital 
quality checking system provided additional assurance for 
high-value products, particularly those supplied to 
automotive customers. This is helping us to stabilise quality 
as we expand circular production routes.

Outlook
Innovation is deeply embedded in how we protect people, 
operate plants, reduce emissions, design materials and 
build capability. In 2026 and beyond, we will continue  
scaling the solutions that showed the strongest traction  
in 2025: AI-enabled safety systems; predictive maintenance; 
environmental intelligence; lower-carbon products;

Environmental performance relies increasingly on early 
detection. Environmental visibility has also become a critical 
element of operational control, particularly as expectations 
rise on air quality and water management performance. 
Diffuse dust emissions and water quality risks require  
early detection and accurate modelling. Our I SEE toolbox 
grew to seven modules, integrating sensors, cameras  
and AI analytics to improve traceability and accuracy  
of environmental data. A solar powered pilot plant 
commissioned at Tubarão, Brazil also began providing 
real time water quality monitoring to strengthen long-term 
water resilience.

We continued testing new ideas at scale and evaluating 
future technology pathways with discipline. AI Augmented 
Product Development (AAPD) further accelerated selected 
automotive and industrial grades, delivering up to a five- 
fold reduction in design time, faster speed to market  
and accelerated patent analysis.

To ensure these tools scale safely and consistently, we 
strengthened the governance structures that support 
digital and AI innovation. The most important governance 
development in 2025 was deepening collaboration between 
global R&D and the Group Chief Technology Office (GCTO) 
– aligning research, innovation and technology functions so 
breakthrough projects on new process technologies are 
steered more systematically and prioritised more effectively.

Alongside these improvements, we also focused on 
accelerating the adoption of AI and digital solutions  
across the sites. Through Accelerated Digital Innovation 
(ADI), we tested scalable digital use cases in six plants, 
Dofasco, Calvert, Pecém, Tubarão-Vega, Piracicaba,  
and Gent – designed for scalability following pilot 
implementation. For example, at ArcelorMittal Dofasco  
in Canada, AI tools are being used to resolve throughput 
bottlenecks in the Coupled Pickle Cold Mill through an 
AI-powered maintenance Q&A interface, predictive 
detection systems to prevent unplanned stoppages,  
and advanced mathematical modelling to optimise  
logistics and operational flow. At ArcelorMittal Pecém  
in Brazil, sensors and cameras integrated with AI-driven  
data and image analysis are enhancing sinter plant 
reliability by anticipating critical failures, thereby reducing 
downtime while improving efficiency and process safety. 
These deployments are creating a model for how digital 
solutions can be replicated quickly and consistently.

Reflecting the growing importance of digital and AI, we 
established a Digital Council, co-sponsored by the GCTO 
and the GCIO, to coordinate AI and digital activity, set 
priorities, enable collaboration and address compliance 
with evolving regulation. A Responsible AI Usage Policy was 
developed in 2025, and we are preparing for new regulations 
including the EU AI Act (entering into force in 2027). In parallel, 
we continued shifting cybersecurity toward a Zero Trust 
model and embedding resilience so AI-driven decisions 
remain safe, accurate and recoverable under stress. Most 
plant level AI solutions operate inside our network, ensuring 
continuity in the event of cloud network issues.

Strategic approach to managing impacts,  
risks and opportunities 
Our strategic approach focuses on identifying, 
assessing, and managing environmental impacts, risks, 
and opportunities (IROs) across our operations. This 
approach places particular emphasis on water-related 
IROs, including water stress and water quality, and on 
pollution-related IROs affecting air, water and soil.

In 2025, the Investment Allocation Committee approved 
capital expenditure totalling US$150 million for 14 projects 
with environmental benefits. 66% of the approved capital 
expenditure related to projects to upgrade air-emissions 
equipment, while 30% related to water projects.

Progress in 2025 
While NOx and SOx intensities show minor year-on-year 
movements, these remain within the normal range of 
operational and statistical variability, reflecting routine 
fluctuations in process conditions and raw material 
characteristics. Overall, performance in both indicators 
has remained broadly stable.

Climate and biodiversity are reported on in separate 
chapters of this report.

The identification and assessment of IROs combine 
site-level expertise with external datasets and recognised 
analytical tools. This enables risk-based prioritisation of 
actions, with a focus on pollution control, water stewardship, 
and compliance-related risks. Resources are allocated to 
locations and activities where these environmental risks 
are most material.

Environmental data governance and reporting systems
In 2025, we strengthened our environmental data governance 
framework to enhance data quality, consistency, traceability 
and auditability across the company.

Governance and regulatory framework
In 2025, we continued to embed environmental sustainability 
as a core component of our business strategy, recognising 
that environmental performance underpins operational 
resilience, regulatory compliance, and long-term value 
creation. Accordingly, our approach is based on robust 
governance, evidence-based decision-making, and 
continuous improvement across our global operations.

For ducted dust emissions and net freshwater consumption, 
the increases observed in the company’s KPIs are primarily 
linked to changes in the reporting scope and production 
mix, rather than a decline in environmental efficiency. 
The inclusion of additional operations that produce 
intermediate products, but not crude steel, affects 
ratio-based indicators such as ducted dust intensity. In 
water consumption, the share of more water intensive 
finished products increased in 2025, influencing group-level 
intensity metrics.

To reflect the differing nature and timescales of 
environmental issues, we assess IROs across multiple 
time horizons:

The Board Sustainability Committee (SC) has oversight of 
environment-related issues. Environmental governance and 
performance is delegated to the local management, with 
accountability for implementation of the strategy and 
compliance with local requirements resting with local CEOs.

ResponsibleSteel™. In 2025, 63% of our mining sites and 95%

## CASE STUDY

Improving environmental performance 
at Fos-sur-Mer, France

In response to the EU Surface Treatment Using Organic 
Coatings BAT reference document regulating volatile 
organic compound (VOC) emissions, we accelerated the 
modernisation of organic coating lines across Europe. 
In 2025, production lines in Poland, Belgium and France were 
equipped with modern abatement technologies, including 
regenerative thermal oxidisers and zeolite wheel systems, 
resulting in significant reductions in VOC emissions. 
We continue preparatory work for the modernisation 
of European finishing lines, with further upgrades 
planned for 2026.

As part of this governance enhancement, we are developing 
and deploying a dedicated IT system for environmental 
data collection and reporting. The system automates data 
submissions, applies automated validation checks, and 
provides audit trails to support internal review and external 
assurance processes. Once implemented, the system will 
reduce reliance on manual data handling and strengthen 
the robustness of reported environmental information.

ArcelorMittal Fos-sur-Mer has implemented a multi-year 
investment programme to strengthen its environmental 
performance and support continued alignment with 
regulatory expectations.

The site also maintains its own internal monitoring systems, 
enabling continuous oversight of emissions and ensuring 
that improvements are sustained over time.

In addition, the site has regular engagements with multiple 
stakeholders on its environmental emissions including local 
authorities, state and employee representatives. Several 
inspections are mandated by DREAL, Direction Régionale 
de l’Environnement, de l’Aménagement et du Logement, 
to verify Fos-sur-Mer compliance with French 
environmental and industrial risk standards.

A central component of this programme is the deployment 
of the Zero Dust at Sinter (0DAS) initiative, implemented 
following a formal notice issued by the competent 
authorities concerning dust emissions at the sinter plant. 
Two advanced filtration units were commissioned in 
2022 and 2023, upgrading the site’s emissions control 
infrastructure. This project received financial support 
from the European Regional Development Fund. Since 
their installation, dust emissions from the associated 
stacks have consistently remained largely below the 
regulatory limit of 30 mg/m3. These results have been 
independently verified through external inspections. 
Based on the improvements implemented and this 
performance, the 2017 notice was subsequently 
lifted by the authorities in October 2023.

The new governance framework supports the timely 
escalation and resolution of data-quality issues and aligns 
environmental reporting practices more closely with the 
level of control applied to financial data. Its implementation 
represents a key step in preparing for increasing 
requirements for data accuracy and assurance.

In Brazil, over US$300 million was invested between 2018 and 
2025 in initiatives to improve atmospheric controls in 
Tubarão. This included the installation of 6.5 km of wind 
fences, upgrades to 42 km of conveyor belts, and 28 new 
de-dusting systems. In addition, more than US$50.8 million 
has been allocated to replace the main de-dusting system 
in the melt shop in Monlevade, which is expected to be 
operational in the first half of 2026.

The cumulative effect of these investments has been 
a substantial reduction in key atmospheric emissions 
between 2015 and 2025:

## –Nitrogen oxides (NOx): reduced by 64%, from

framework and prepare for forthcoming regulatory

on compliance with evolving air quality and pollution control 
requirements and on preparing for more stringent EU air 
quality standards applicable from 2030. We prioritised 
targeted investments in emissions reduction and pollution 
control measures to support current compliance and 
long-term regulatory resilience.

In parallel, Fos-sur-Mer completed a €150 million 
modernisation of the site’s coke plant, including the full 
refurbishment of its 126 furnaces. The final phase of this 
project, delivered in 2022, introduced an automatic safety 
valve ignition system across the plant’s 28 chimneys. 
This system ensures that, in the event of an operational 
incident, coke oven gases are safely combusted rather 
than released directly into the atmosphere, thereby 
reducing the potential environmental impact of isolated 
events and enhancing overall process safety.

## CASE STUDY

Dust reduction project  
at ArcelorMittal  
Long Products in

Water quality monitoring and pollution prevention
In addition to managing water volumes, we monitor water 
quality to limit potential impacts on ecosystems and 
downstream users. Water pollutant monitoring is conducted 
at most operational sites, in line with permit requirements 
and local regulations. Depending on site-specific conditions, 
monitored parameters may include suspended solids, total 
organic carbon and other relevant substances.

Water stewardship and water stress management
Water plays an essential role in steel manufacturing and 
finishing processes, supporting cooling, gas cleaning, 
washing and waste-gas treatment. Although steelmaking 
requires substantial volumes of water, overall net 
consumption remains relatively low due to high levels 
of recirculation and reuse.

Nevertheless, increasing water scarcity and rising 
regulatory pressures make sustainable water management 
a growing priority, both to safeguard production reliability 
and to support fair access to water for other users in the 
same regions.

Where required, water-treatment systems are operated to 
ensure that discharge quality meets applicable standards. 
Monitoring results are reviewed at site level and inform 
continuous improvement measures to reduce pollution risks 
and protect receiving water bodies.

In Differdange, a major dust reduction project 
illustrates how innovation, reuse, and rigorous project 
management can deliver substantial environmental 
benefits, including improved air quality for employees, 
residents and other stakeholders.

Launched in December 2023, the project’s first phase 
involved the dismantling, transporting, and reinstalling of 
a dust collection unit from Hayange, located 30 km away. 
The equipment had not been designed for relocation or 
upcycling, making the operation technically complex. 
Despite this, the work was completed safely, within the 
planned schedule and within the allocated budget.

This approach is applied across our operations, including 
at ArcelorMittal Monlevade, Brazil. Between 2021 and 2025, 
the site implemented a robust water management 
enhancement programme focused on strengthening 
treatment systems and advancing operational excellence. 
Key initiatives included significant investment in the 
cleaning, modernisation, and optimisation of the Monlevade 
water reuse station, as well as the reinforcement of monthly 
water quality monitoring.

Operational water efficiency and freshwater use reduction
In 2025, we placed particular emphasis on reducing 
freshwater consumption in our Flat Europe segment 
through rainwater collection and enhanced internal 
recycling. In Avilés (Spain), a project was commissioned 
to enable efficient rainwater collection, protect soil from 
pollution, and, in a later phase, recycle collected water 
within production processes. The project involves the 
collection of rainwater from the industrial areas of the 
Avilés plant to prevent pollution of a small river running 
through the site. The collected water is currently routed to 
the industrial process wastewater collector. In Saint-Chély 
(France), a large-capacity rainwater storage tank was 
installed, allowing rainwater to be reused for internal process 
needs and reducing reliance on freshwater sources. This 
measure supports France’s national objective to reduce 
water withdrawals by 10% by 2030. In Sagunto (Spain), 
a project is underway to enhance wastewater discharge 
quality by reducing metal concentrations. In Gent (Belgium), 
we are working on increasing wastewater treatment 
capacity for the entire site.

By mid-2025, the system was fully installed and 
operational in Differdange. The project has now entered 
its second phase, focusing on connecting the main 
emission sources, including the new black-slag hall, 
ladle furnace and ladle tumbler, using custom-designed 
large-scale hoods and ducting.

In 2025, a detailed water assessment confirmed the 
effectiveness of the measures implemented, demonstrating 
that site operations were in line with best environmental 
management practices and the protection of local 
water bodies, including the Piracicaba River and the 
Carneirinhos stream.

Once commissioning and calibration are completed, 
the system is projected to deliver an 80% reduction 
in diffuse dust emissions by the end of 2026.

As part of this structured programme, the ReNascentes 
project was initiated to restore 20 natural water springs 
within the plant’s operational area of influence. Over a 
three-year period, the project delivered actions such as 
waste removal, area fencing, and native tree planting. 
These activities were led by a multidisciplinary team 
of ArcelorMittal employees from the environmental, 
infrastructure, and forest fire prevention teams, reinforcing 
the company’s dedication to natural resource conservation 
and employee engagement.

We also focused on our Vanderbijlpark (South Africa) site, 
which faced water imbalances following a coke battery 
shutdown during 2024/2025. Mitigation measures were 
required to restore balance and achieve zero effluent 
discharge. This was achieved through enhanced separation

## Planet – Strengthening our environmental performance continued

Water stress risk assessment and mitigation
Water stress is assessed annually across all sites using 
third-party climate data and insights from the World 
Resources Institute’s Aqueduct Water Risk Atlas, which 
defines water stress as the ratio of total water withdrawals 
to available renewable annual water flow. This approach 
provides a consistent, science-based understanding of 
current and future water-related risks.

Through targeted optimisation projects and process 
adjustments, the Florange plant has achieved a reduction 
of more than 50% in water consumption, equivalent to 
approximately 5.4 million m3 per year, generating estimated 
annual savings of around €761,000. These improvements 
enabled the site to avoid production constraints during 
drought periods and contributed to the achievement of 
ResponsibleSteel™ certification. The methodology and 
technologies applied have proven scalable and replicable, 
with similar systems assessed at Dunkirk in 2025, where 
feasibility was confirmed including complex configurations 
such as underground piping and bidirectional flows. The 
Florange experience demonstrates how data-driven water 
management can deliver substantial environmental, 
operational and financial benefits.

Master Plan in 2014, focused on further reducing freshwater 
dependence, expanding alternative water sources and 
strengthening recirculation infrastructure.

Key milestones under the plan include the modernisation 
of the water treatment plant in 2016, the development of 
sanitary effluent reuse solutions, the implementation of the 
Project Springs initiative to protect and restore local water 
sources, the commissioning of a seawater desalination plant 
in 2021 and the introduction of the HidroES system in 2023. 
HidroES provides real-time monitoring and short-term 
forecasting of river-flow conditions, supporting more 
informed operational decision-making during periods of 
hydrological stress. Together, these measures have reduced 
freshwater consumption drawn from shared community 
sources by more than 35% and strengthened long-term 
regional water security. Future actions will focus on further 
expanding alternative water sources and limiting freshwater 
use to essential drinking-water needs, reinforcing the site’s 
commitment to anticipating resource constraints while 
maintaining operational stability.

21 of our sites operate in regions identified as having 
material water risk or high-water stress conditions, including 
locations in South Africa, Belgium, Mexico, Morocco and 
Brazil. Understanding local water conditions enables the 
prioritisation of targeted water stewardship actions, 
ensuring that mitigation measures align with site-specific 
risks, regulatory expectations and long-term climate trends.

A similarly proactive and long-term approach has been 
implemented at ArcelorMittal Tubarão in Brazil, where water 
resource management has long been a strategic priority. 
Approximately 96% of cooling water at the site is drawn from 
the sea and returned after circulation, while only around 
4% of total water consumption comes from freshwater 
supplied by the local utility. More than 97% of this freshwater 
is recirculated and reused. In response to increasing regional 
water scarcity, the site launched a comprehensive Water

In regions facing persistent or increasing water constraints, 
sites may implement a range of measures to strengthen 
water resilience. These include improving water efficiency 
and repairing system leaks, expanding water reuse and 
recirculation across processes, sourcing water from 
alternative supplies such as desalinated water where 
appropriate, and capturing rainwater for non-potable 
applications. Where risks are expected to intensify over 
time based on available assessments, several sites have 
adopted comprehensive Water Master Plans to 
systematically reduce freshwater dependency and 
anticipate potential regulatory or physical constraints.

Our Belval site in Luxembourg operates a semi-
closed water circuit with a primary water reserve 
pond (Étang Laminoir) and a secondary pond 
(Étang Haut-Fourneau).

In 2023, a detailed monitoring campaign was 
conducted to analyse pump activation between 
the two ponds and associated overflow volumes. 
The assessment concluded that the pump activation 
parameters were not aligned with the plant’s 
operational needs. The operational team 
subsequently revised the water-level sensor 
settings and pump activation thresholds, leading 
to a substantial reduction in pumped volumes 
and associated water losses.

In France, increasing water scarcity and heightened 
regulatory restrictions prompted the launch of a Water 
Master Plan at ArcelorMittal Florange (Flat Europe) in 2019. 
The initiative was designed to proactively reduce freshwater 
consumption, strengthen drought resilience, and anticipate 
potential water-use restrictions of up to 25% during crisis 
periods imposed by French authorities. The approach 
combined enhanced governance, real-time monitoring, and 
the systematic integration of water efficiency considerations 
into all new projects and process modifications. Low-cost, 
state-of-the-art wireless technologies were deployed to 
connect both existing and new flowmeters, enabling 
real-time data collection, aggregation and integration into 
a single digital platform. This significantly improved visibility 
into water consumption patterns, leak detection, and 
operational deviations.

Whereas annual pumped volumes previously 
ranged from approximately 22,000m3 to 105,000m3 
prior to 2024, the total volume pumped since the 
implementation of the revised parameters has been 
reduced to approximately 5,400m3 for the 2024/2025 
period. This initiative demonstrates how continuous 
monitoring and targeted adjustments to control 
parameters can improve water efficiency, reduce 
unnecessary water transfers, and lower operational 
costs without requiring additional infrastructure.

## Planet – Strengthening our environmental performance continued

Building on our circular economy commitments
The circularity of steel is already deeply embedded in 
our business model. Steel is endlessly recyclable, and its 
recycling rates are amongst the highest of any material. 
As part of our wider environmental commitments, our 
stakeholders want to see reduced impacts from steelmaking 
and its supply chain, and at the same time we have an 
inherent business interest and opportunity in producing 
steel more efficiently, with fewer virgin material inputs and 
reduced waste generation. For us, circularity in the steel 
industry goes well beyond simply waste management and 
recycling; it encompasses the whole life cycle of steel and 
its raw materials, from extraction and sourcing to product 
design, use, reuse, remanufacturing and end-of-life 
recovery. Our principal approach to circularity as a business 
is to address the three R’s of Reduce, Reuse and Recycle:

In Brazil, the integration of digital innovation and advanced 
analytics has also enabled further progress in climate 
resilience and water security at ArcelorMittal Brazil Long. 
Building on site-level water stewardship priorities, we 
developed a Basin Predictability Study in partnership with 
the Federal University of Juiz de Fora (UFJF), resulting in a 
new industrial water security assessment model. The model 
integrates internal water-use data with external river basin 
analyses and climate change scenarios to provide a 
comprehensive view of long-term water availability and risk.

## CASE STUDY

Rehabilitation of the Yekepa Sewage Treatment
