Over the past year, AML has completed a major 
rehabilitation works at the Yekepa Sewage Treatment 
Plant. Upgrades included an additional biological 
treatment chamber, new clarification and storage units, 
improved screening systems, and tertiary treatment with 
filtration and disinfection. These improvements are 
expected to enhance water quality and long-term 
operational reliability.

To support affected communities, AML has constructed 
two solar-powered water kiosks providing safe, treated 
water. The company has also funded an ecological 
restoration and livelihood programme worth US$84,000. 
The programme was implemented through the 
Committee for Peace and Development Advocacy 
(COPDA), supporting wetland restoration and 
sustainable farming.

The project’s main outcome was the development of 
a digital platform capable of monitoring water availability 
in near real time, calculating water balances, and 
generating forecasts over 10-, 20-, and 30-year horizons. 
By combining traditional hydrological modelling with 
advanced AI techniques, the system translates complex 
hydrological variables into operational indicators. This 
supports the anticipation of extreme events such as 
droughts and floods, supports informed decision-making, 
and helps prioritise investments in climate resilience, water 
reuse and alternative water sources.

Most of our R&D programmes on mining circularity focus 
on industrial symbiosis — that is, the reuse of tailings, 
or fractions of them as secondary materials for other 
industries. Applications may include use in cement, 
concrete, bricks or tiles. In some countries, we are also 
researching specific applications to recover critical raw 
materials from tailings. We are also exploring opportunities 
to use tailings-derived minerals to create fertile soils for 
internal mining-land rehabilitation, which may support 
local biodiversity enhancement.

Where feasible, we are establishing closed‑loop business 
models with strategic partners, including the logistics 
required to recover end‑of‑life products and recycle them 
into new steel solutions. One example is ArcelorMittal 
Building Solutions’ investment in a state-of-the-art recycling 
unit for polyurethane (PU) foam sandwich panels, which 
supports greater circularity in the construction sector. The 
initiative is designed to enable the effective recycling of 
both the insulation core and the steel components of these 
commonly used building-system components. The new 
technology specifically targets PU-foam sandwich panels, 
which are widely used in roofing and façade systems. 
By enabling the recovery and reuse of these materials 
at the end of their life-cycle, the project supports a circular 
economy approach, reducing waste and limiting reliance 
on virgin raw materials. This represents an important step 
towards more sustainable construction practices, 
particularly for insulated building systems that have 
historically been challenging to recycle.

Looking ahead, global scrap availability is expected 
to increase as the volume of steel in circulation continues 
to grow, demonstrating the inherent circularity of steel. 
Our recycling businesses — including John Lawrie 
(Scotland, 2022), Alba International Recycling (Germany, 
2022), and Riwald Recycling (Netherlands, 2023) — illustrate 
how we are strengthening our access to scrap in order to 
support lower-carbon steelmaking and scale up circular 
material flows.

We are increasingly integrating circular economy principles 
into our product innovation and design. The buildings and 
construction sector accounts for around 40% of global 
energy-related carbon emissions, and the industry is 
increasingly looking for low-carbon structures, efficient 
construction methods, low-maintenance building solutions 
and recyclable components. Low-carbon emission steel is 
well positioned to deliver the circularity principles that 
architects, engineers, and developers are looking for.

Where possible, we are seeking to reduce waste materials 
and general refuse across the organisation, thereby 
reducing waste management, storage and detrimental land 
use, while increasingly identifying circular or alternative uses 
for operational by-products. We are working towards a 
‘zero waste to landfill’ goal as part of our broader circular 
economy strategy.

Enhancing tailings 
management safety

Consideration of international principles
Our Tailings Management System draws on internationally 
recognised best practices to support safety, transparency, 
and environmental protection. It draws on the leading 
guidelines and standards from the Mining Association of 
Canada (MAC), which provides protocols for responsible 
tailings design and operation; the Canadian Dam 
Association (CDA), which sets rigorous requirements for 
dam safety and structural integrity; and the Global Industry 
Standard on Tailings Management (GISTM), the most 
comprehensive global benchmark developed to prevent 
catastrophic failures.

A key area of focus for us going forward is strengthening 
alignment of all our tailings storage facilities with the 
Australian National Committee on Large Dams (ANCOLD) 
guidelines, widely regarded as global best practice for 
dam safety and risk management.

iSMART (Integrated Surveillance and Monitoring 
ArcelorMittal Technologies) 
iSMART (Integrated Surveillance and Monitoring 
ArcelorMittal Technologies) is an in-house cloud-based, 
GIS-enabled system that incorporates multiple data 
sources coming from on-site sensors, surveys, satellite 
imaging, and other sources to create custom monitoring 
dashboards and alert systems in real time for our  
tailings dams.

## We currently manage 26 TSFs, which are categorised as:

Tailings are the residual materials left after mined iron ore 
has been processed and the valuable iron extracted. They 
typically consist of finely ground rock, water, and by-
products such as silica, alumina and residual iron particles. 
To prevent these materials from contaminating rivers, 
groundwater, and surrounding ecosystems, they are stored 
in specially engineered containment structures called 
Tailings Storage Facilities (TSFs). These facilities are 
designed with the intention of helping to keep tailings 
secure and to support the protection of people, wildlife 
and water resources.

## – Active: 16

## – Dormant: 2

## – Care & maintenance: 4

It is designed by ArcelorMittal R&D for tailings dam safety 
monitoring and management. Deployed in Azure Cloud,  
it is used to gather, process, organise and visualise all 
relevant physical data that are essential to monitoring 
tailings dam stability. It triggers alerts where stability 
might be compromised, enabling timely remedial action.

## – In closure process: 1

## – Under construction: 3

GISTM is built around six key principles that guide all stages 
of tailings management. It emphasises the protection and 
involvement of affected communities, prioritising their safety 
and rights. It requires an integrated knowledge base that 
combines geological, engineering, and environmental data 
to understand and manage risks. The standard calls for 
best-in-class practices in design, construction, operations, 
and continuous monitoring throughout the facility’s life 
cycle. Strong management and governance are essential, 
with clear accountability at the highest organisational 
levels for tailings safety. GISTM also mandates robust 
emergency response and long-term recovery plans to 
restore ecosystems and communities if an incident occurs. 
Finally, it promotes transparency through public disclosure 
and access to information, enabling stakeholders to 
understand and trust how tailings risks are managed.

As part of our ongoing safety programme, tailings 
specialists are embedded within operations, and all TSFs 
have emergency response procedures activated by Trigger 
Action Response Plans (TARPs). We have been reviewing 
these response plans across all sites to ensure they are 
aligned with the international best practice standards.

iSMART strengthens operational oversight by centralising 
and automating data integration and analysis, ensuring 
consistent processes across all mining sites. It improves 
visibility of tailings dam dynamics, increases the accuracy 
of risk assessments, and strengthens environmental and 
safety compliance.

Historically, most TSFs have stored wet tailings—a slurry 
of water and fine particles—which pose a higher risk of 
structural failure and catastrophic flooding. To reduce this 
risk, the industry is increasingly adopting dry stack tailings, 
where tailings are dewatered and compacted, making them 
more stable and significantly less hazardous.

This work is supported by a continuous improvement 
programme that promotes reduced moisture disposal 
methodologies, which improve the structural integrity of 
tailings dams, (e.g. high-density thickened tailings or filtered 
tailings where appropriate). It also leverages proven new 
technologies to monitor facilities globally in real time. We 
have upgraded our internal monitoring infrastructure by 
deploying iSMART, a new cloud-based TSF data-monitoring 
system. This Geographic Information System (GIS) based 
monitoring platform integrates all safety data, sourced from 
on-site instrumentation, such as piezometers and 
inclinometers that measure deformation, and remote 
monitoring technologies like InSAR satellite imaging. This 
provides both real-time and predictive assessments through 
regular, scheduled, high-precision data and imagery to 
track mass movement in millimetre increments over time, 
enabling us to detect very small changes in site morphology 
and trends that then drive our decision-making and actions.

Based on its success, iSMART is now being extended to 
other areas of our mining operations, such as open pit 
water table monitoring and trigger action response plans 
(TARP) for pit stability, with AI models that predict sensor 
values based on seasonality and other trends.

When a TSF fails, the consequences can be severe – large 
volumes of water and tailings can flood surrounding areas, 
causing environmental devastation, loss of life, property 
destruction, and long-term contamination of land and rivers. 
Past failures worldwide have underscored the catastrophic 
impacts of poor design and oversight. Today, effective 
tailings management is more critical than ever, driven by 
the growing scale of mining operations, climate-related 
challenges, and heightened expectations from regulators, 
investors, and communities for transparency and safety.

By taking into consideration these standards, we aim to 
minimise environmental impact, protect communities, and 
maintain the highest level of operational integrity.

Progress in 2025
In 2025, we conducted a full third-party GISTM audit 
of all our TSFs, and completed a significant set of 
quantitative risk assessments as part of the ongoing 
risk management strategy.

At ArcelorMittal, tailings dam safety and structural integrity 
are critical and material issues, essential to protecting the 
safety of our local communities and employees and 
safeguarding the environment from pollution and flooding. 
Our aim is to ensure all our TSFs are structurally sound and 
safe, through rigorous risk assessment, independent audits 
and reviews benchmarked against international guidelines 
and comprehensive tailings monitoring procedures and 
action plans.

Engagement with the community 
Community and stakeholder engagement is an important 
part of our broader tailings management approach, 
aligning directly with GISTM Principle 1 relating to Affected 
Communities. ArcelorMittal’s approach to managing risks 
for affected communities is anchored in our Human Rights 
Policy and Local External Stakeholder Engagement 
Procedure, both of which are mandatory across all 
operations. Our Human Rights Policy sets our approach to 
respecting internationally recognised rights, including those 
outlined in the UN Universal Declaration of Human Rights, 
ILO conventions, and the UN Guiding Principles on Business 
and Human Rights. It guides us to identify, prevent, and 
mitigate potential adverse impacts on people throughout 
our activities.

## ArcelorMittal Brazil, Serra Azul tailings dam: a responsible closure approach

Emergency preparedness remains a core pillar of 
the programme. The activation of the PAEBM in 2019 
involved a precautionary evacuation carried out in 
coordination with local authorities, based on updated 
technical assessments. Each year, ArcelorMittal Brazil 
and the Municipal Civil Defence Authority conduct a 
comprehensive Level 3 emergency drill, testing siren 
activation along the Self-Rescue Zone (ZAS), the 
operation of 13 road control points, and the evacuation 
of more than 120 people who work on maintenance 
activities within the ZAS, following designated escape 
routes to 28 assembly points. The exercise forms part 
of the PAEBM’s mandatory Conformity and Operability 
Assessment (ACO), in accordance with National Mining 
Agency’s (ANM) Resolution No. 95/2022, reinforcing 
preparedness for high-consequence, low-probability 
scenarios. These drills strengthen the region’s emergency 
response culture, reinforce the company’s safety 
standards for the community and ensure continuous 
improvement in coordination with public authorities.

The Serra Azul iron ore mine in Itatiaiuçu, Minas Gerais, 
has been a major part of Brazil’s Iron Quadrangle since 
mining began in 1946, long before ArcelorMittal’s 
ownership. Originally operated by Minas Itatiaiuçu and 
later modernised by London Mining, the site was acquired 
by ArcelorMittal in 2008. Situated within a dense mining 
corridor, Serra Azul is bordered by long-established 
Mineração Usiminas (MUSA) operations – Mina Oeste, 
Mina Central and Mina Leste e Minerita – Mineração 
Itaúna – which have extracted and processed iron ore 
in the same ridge system for decades, creating a region 
shaped by interlinked infrastructure, environmental 
considerations and community expectations.

In August 2025, ArcelorMittal Brazil obtained its 
environmental licence to begin the full deconstruction 
of the structure. This resumption of licensing followed the 
completion of the Downstream Containment Structure 
(ECJ) – an engineered barrier built specifically to retain 
the entire mass of tailings in a breach scenario, as required 
under Brazil’s reinforced post-Brumadinho regulations. 
The deconstruction process will follow a carefully 
sequenced approach, including controlled water 
drainage, structural stabilisation and the use of remotely 
operated equipment during higher-risk activities, with 
completion expected in 2032.

Complementing this, our Local External Stakeholder 
Engagement Procedure mandates inclusive and transparent 
dialogue with local communities, enabling us to understand 
concerns, incorporate feedback and build trust. Together, 
these frameworks form a critical part of our risk management 
process, helping us safeguard community well-being 
and uphold our commitment to responsible and 
sustainable operations.

ArcelorMittal Brazil’s approach emphasises layered 
protection for communities and the environment. Since 
2019, the dam has been subject to continuous risk 
assessment under PAEBM protocols, including early warning 
systems, stability monitoring and emergency readiness 
procedures. Engineering enhancements – structural 
reinforcements, drainage improvements and advanced 
instrumentation – align with regulatory requirements and 
international good practice. Controlled decommissioning 
techniques further reduce environmental risks while 
supporting long-term remediation.

The tailings dam at Serra Azul has been inactive since 
2012, following the site’s shift to dry stacking. In 2019, the 
dam entered Brazil’s Emergency Action Plan for Mining 
Dams (PAEBM), being reclassified from emergency level 2 
to level 3 in compliance with Brazilian legislation. Since 
then, the company has implemented a comprehensive 
set of 24/7 geotechnical monitoring measures, which have 
maintained stable safety indicators. ArcelorMittal Brazil 
strengthened oversight with real-time monitoring 
technologies – including piezometers, inclinometers and 
seismic sensors – to track stability conditions and inform 
preventive engineering interventions.

Priorities for 2026
In 2026, we will focus on continuing to embed global best 
practice standards into everyday operations, strengthening 
predictive monitoring and early warning systems so risks are 
identified and addressed well before they escalate, and 
maintaining strong governance, emergency preparedness, 
and transparent engagement with affected communities.

In 2025, members of ArcelorMittal’s Board and 
Sustainability Committee visited Serra Azul as part of their 
governance oversight responsibilities. The visit enabled 
directors to meet with engineering and community 
relations teams, understand the functioning of the ECJ 
and monitoring systems, and observe how emergency 
preparedness, safety procedures and engagement 
efforts are implemented on the ground.

ArcelorMittal Brazil continues to provide assistance to 
residents affected by the 2019 precautionary evacuation, 
including monthly financial support, relocation to new 
homes, psychosocial services, care for evacuated animals, 
maintenance of properties located in the high safety zone 
(ZAS), dedicated dialogue and communication channels, as 
part of formal agreements with the authorities. In 2025, the 
Collective Reparation Agreement was signed, amounting 
to R$ 440 million, which funds approximately 65 measures – 
including infrastructure, social and economic programmes 
– that benefit the entire municipality of Itatiaiuçu. These 
actions acknowledge the impacts experienced by the 
communities of Pinheiros, Vieiras and Lagoa das Flores 
and prioritise their wellbeing throughout the social 
reparation process and the dam deconstruction.

As decommissioning advances towards its planned 
completion in 2032, ArcelorMittal Brazil will continue to 
apply rigorous monitoring, maintain open dialogue with 
communities and work closely with authorities to ensure 
a safe, transparent and environmentally responsible 
closure. The Serra Azul programme represents more 
than the technical retirement of a legacy structure – 
it illustrates the company’s commitment to protecting 
people, safeguarding the environment and strengthening 
trust across one of Brazil’s most intensively mined regions.

Planet 
Safeguarding biodiversity 
and ecosystems

Through the LEAP assessments, we identified potential 
impacts such as dust emissions, water discharges, and 
habitat fragmentation, alongside positive contributions like 
long-standing green-belt initiatives and habitat protection 
measures. These efforts have supported local biodiversity 
and provided buffers that reduce environmental pressures 
on surrounding communities.

We recognise stakeholder concerns about land use and 
biodiversity and are working on enhancing our reporting on 
how we manage this issue.

We have recognised the need to enhance our capacity 
in this area and incorporate TNFD’s LEAP methodology into 
our approach. This structured process enables us to:

Respect for nature and sustainable development
Biodiversity is dynamic and often influenced by factors beyond 
our control. Our approach is adaptive, evidence-based, 
and guided by international standards. By embedding 
biodiversity considerations into governance, planning, and 
operations, we aim to reduce risks, capture opportunities, 
and contribute positively to the resilience of ecosystems 
and communities surrounding our priority locations.

## – Locate biodiversity-sensitive areas and ecosystem

service dependencies
	
– Evaluate potential impacts and pressures

## – Assess material risks and opportunities

Protecting biodiversity and ecosystems inherently 
supports the stability of the natural environment, habitats 
and species. It is an important contributor to the resilience 
of our operations and the well-being of the surrounding 
local communities, providing essential ecosystem services 
– such as freshwater supply, climate regulation, and flood 
mitigation – that support our business and our social 
licence to operate. Our approach is structured and 
adaptive, embedding biodiversity considerations into 
governance, planning and operations.

The assessments also revealed dependencies that 
are material to our operations—freshwater availability, 
vegetation buffers, and coastal ecosystems for flood 
resilience. Identified risks include regulatory exposure, 
physical risks linked to climate change, and reputational 
considerations. Opportunities include nature-based 
solutions for flood management and partnerships with 
conservation and community organisations.

## – Prepare responses that integrate biodiversity into

decision-making.

LEAP provides a consistent framework for prioritising sites 
and building a robust evidence base for disclosure and 
action. Site selection processes screen for proximity to 
protected areas, key biodiversity areas, and threatened 
species. We seek to avoid operations in areas of highest 
biodiversity value wherever feasible, and apply the 
mitigation hierarchy – avoid, minimise, restore, offset – 
across project planning and operations, consistent with 
international standards.

For investors, this means a stronger foundation for long-term 
value creation. For customers, it reflects our approach to 
responsible sourcing and environmental stewardship. For 
employees, it demonstrates that we are working to build a 
business that respects nature and supports sustainable 
development. We are evaluating approaches to contribute 
to no net loss of biodiversity at priority sites, informed by 
global best practice and stakeholder expectations.

At the local level, ArcelorMittal Brazil has established a 
national biodiversity working group to map and manage 
the impacts of operations on biodiversity across all its 
priority industrial and forestry sites. The group also works 
to understand the interdependence between operational 
activities and ecosystem services, integrating this 
perspective into its decision-making. It has aligned its 
internal guidelines with global best practices such as TNFD 
and the principles of ‘no net loss’1 and positive impact.

We are using the Task Force for Nature-related Financial 
Disclosure (TNFD) LEAP (Locate, Evaluate, Assess, Prepare) 
methodology to identify site-specific impacts, dependencies, 
and risks across priority steel and mining locations. 
Looking ahead, we are developing a roadmap to 
strengthen biodiversity outcomes by expanding site-level 
assessments, improving indicators for habitat and species 
condition, and engaging value-chain partners to align with 
emerging standards.

We seek to minimise operational footprints, progressively 
rehabilitate, consult with communities, and form 
partnerships with local NGOs and biodiversity experts to 
co-develop restoration and monitoring programmes.

Governance and assurance in line with international 
best practice
Our governance framework integrates biodiversity 
into environmental policy and management systems. 
Oversight sits within our environmental governance structure, 
supported by site-level systems certified to ISO 14001 and 
complemented by third-party programmes such as 
ResponsibleSteel™ and the Forest Stewardship Council (FSC). 
These certifications provide independent assurance of our 
asset-based approaches and require biodiversity 
management measures, including site-specific plans 
and mitigation actions.

Progress in 2025
Based on initial LEAP pilot assessments at Bremen (steel) 
and Liberia (mining) to test our methodology and approach, 
we completed three additional LEAP assessments in Brazil, 
focusing on priority sites in steelmaking and mining. The 
pilots confirmed the value of combining primary data, such 
as site biodiversity surveys, with secondary sources like 
global species databases and geospatial tools. We benefit 
from our membership in the United Nations Environment 
Programme Proteus Partnership in accessing and using 
valuable global datasets, notably the Integrated 
Biodiversity Assessment Tool (IBAT).

The working group has also consolidated diagnostics and 
action plans to address identified gaps, conformed with 
ResponsibleSteel™ requirements, promoted environmental 
education and strengthened corporate reputation and 
business resilience through ongoing stakeholder 
engagement. In addition, it has integrated national and 
global targets – such as CONABIO (Comissão Nacional 
de Biodiversidade, Brazil’s National Diversity Commission) 
Resolution No. 9/2024 and the Global Biodiversity 
Framework from COP15 – into internal strategies.

Why biodiversity matters 
Healthy ecosystems are an important factor in the resilience 
of our operations and surrounding communities. They 
provide services we depend on every day – freshwater for 
cooling and processing, vegetation buffers that reduce dust 
and noise, and natural systems that regulate climate and 
mitigate flood risk. These services are fundamental to 
operational continuity and to maintaining our social 
licence to operate.

All major capital projects undergo environmental impact 
assessments, and biodiversity considerations are embedded 
where required by regulation or voluntary standards. This 
supports the early identification of biodiversity risks for 
management through appropriate mitigation strategies.

Stakeholder expectations for biodiversity stewardship 
are continuing to increase. The launch of TNFD’s 
recommendations have accelerated the need for 
transparent, credible disclosures on nature-related risks and 
opportunities. Investors and customers expect companies 
to demonstrate how they manage these issues, while 
employees see biodiversity as part of our broader approach 
to sustainability and responsible growth.

These assessments reinforced our understanding of the 
complexity of biodiversity management. Our varied sites 
operate in diverse landscapes that include native forests, 
wetlands, and coastal ecosystems. These areas deliver 
critical services such as water regulation, flood mitigation, 
and air quality improvement. They also host species of 
conservation concern, underscoring the need for careful 
planning and monitoring.

Strategic approach guided by TNFD
The launch of the TNFD reporting guidelines has 
accelerated and guided the assessment of nature-related 
impacts, risks, and opportunities for business reporting.

1.	 The principle of ‘no net loss’ means that any negative 
environmental or social impacts caused by an activity are fully 
avoided, minimised, restored, or compensated so that there is no 
overall reduction in value compared to the original baseline.

## CASE STUDY

## Safeguarding biodiversity at a Mediterranean trade gateway

Within the site boundary, the northern natural areas are 
the most ecologically significant, forming the core of the 
convergence between Crau drylands and Camargue 
marsh systems. Spring‑fed ponds support rich amphibian 
communities, while surrounding wet meadows host 
specialist flora and invertebrates. Several old buildings 
provide undisturbed conditions for potential bat maternity 
colonies – among the site’s highest‑value faunal assets.

ArcelorMittal’s Fos‑sur‑Mer, France occupies a uniquely 
sensitive position in the Provence region, lying at the 
meeting point of three major ecological systems: the dry, 
stony Crau steppe, the water‑rich wetlands of the 
Camargue, and the marine environment of the Golfe 
de Fos. Few places in southern France host such a direct 
overlap of terrestrial, brackish and marine habitats. This 
creates an ecological crossroads where steppe species, 
wetland specialists and coastal or marine fauna converge 
within a mosaic of reedbeds, spring‑fed ponds, grasslands, 
canals, marshes and coastal waters. From the outset, 
substantial areas of land were intentionally reserved for 
nature, and today around 450 hectares are managed 
under long‑term ecological plans first established in 
1996 and strengthened through collaboration with 
Eco‑Med since 2013.

Seasonal water‑level regulation, targeted brush 
management and the systematic removal of invasive 
plants help maintain habitat structure and prevent the 
encroachment of vegetation that would diminish 
ecological diversity. Nearby lagoons and salt marshes 
support more than 180 bird species – including flamingos, 
avocets, herons and several tern species protected under 
the EU Birds Directive – while Camargue horses and wild 
boar contribute to maintaining vegetation dynamics and 
natural soil processes.

Insights from the Brazil assessments have informed our wider 
biodiversity approach. Using LEAP, we have developed a 
process that combines site-level data with global datasets, 
creating a consistent approach adaptable across regions, 
geographies and operational contexts.

In 2025, we engaged with operations in multiple regions to 
validate the methodology and ensure its relevance to both 
steelmaking and mining. This engagement confirmed that 
biodiversity risks and dependencies vary significantly by 
location, reinforcing the need for tailored action plans 
supported by a common framework.

Fos‑sur‑Mer operates both as a modern trade hub and 
a biodiversity anchor, safeguarding rare habitats and 
ecological processes at one of the Mediterranean’s most 
important and historic ecological and anthropogenic 
crossroads. Controlled access, biodiversity monitoring, 
adaptive habitat management and coordination with 
local angling and hunting groups show how active 
stewardship can sustain sensitive species and habitats 
from surrounding pressures within one of Europe’s most 
strategic trade hubs.

Biodiversity management on the site is guided by rigorous, 
long‑term scientific monitoring carried out by Eco‑Med, 
which tracks flora, invertebrates, amphibians, reptiles, 
birds and mammals across the natural areas. Annual 
surveys identify priority species and habitat trends, 
ensuring that management actions are evidence‑based. 
This includes maintaining temporary pond systems, 
monitoring bat corridors, and protecting rare annual flora 
from shrub and rush encroachment. Regular inventories 
confirm high‑value species such as the Western 
Spadefoot Toad, European Pond Turtle, several notable 
damselflies and grasshoppers, and a broad assemblage 
of wetland birds. These findings underscore the 
conservation value of the Fos site within a heavily 
industrialised landscape.

Priorities for 2026 
Our focus for the coming year and beyond includes:

This crossroads has also shaped human activity for 
millennia. The Rhône delta has long served as a gateway 
between sea and continent – a historic triangle of 
exchange linking Fos‑sur‑Mer, Arles and Le Grau‑du‑Roi. 
Romans cultivated delta lands, developed early salt 
marshes and used the Rhône as a major commercial 
corridor, while Arles emerged as a key trading hub due 
to its position at the meeting point of land, river and sea. 
Through the 19th century, as trade intensified, the State 
financed large‑scale hydraulic engineering to contain, 
drain and manage the delta, reinforcing its strategic 
role in commerce, agriculture and navigation. Today, 
this legacy continues in the modern industrial‑port 
complex of Marseille‑Fos, which serves as France’s 
leading port by tonnage and a major southern 
gateway for European logistics.

1.	  Developing a more harmonised group-wide approach to 
biodiversity management practices and leveraging good 
examples to align more closely with TNFD.
2. 	 Expanding LEAP assessments to additional high-priority 
sites to inform site-specific action plans and compliance 
strategies.
3. 	 Strengthening indicators and monitoring systems for 
habitat condition, species presence, and ecosystem 
service dependencies.
4. 	 Seeking to define biodiversity management targets 
aligned with TNFD guidance and emerging regulations.
5.	  Engaging key value chain partners to address 
biodiversity impacts in raw material sourcing and 
promote good practice across the supply chain.

Management actions are applied continuously 
throughout the year. Invasive plants – particularly 
Baccharis and Water Primrose – are controlled through 
manual removal, canal‑edge management and 
controlled burning in partnership with local authorities. 
Seasonal restrictions on heavy works avoid breeding 
periods for sensitive fauna, while coordinated water‑level 
management maintains key ecological functions for 
amphibians and wetland flora. Collaboration with angling 
and hunting groups further supports compatible land use 
while keeping disturbance low.

Ecologically, the site supports an exceptionally rich 
set of habitats shaped by this same land–water–sea 
interface. Temporary Mediterranean ponds, reedbeds, 
wet grasslands and salt‑influenced flats host 
numerous protected and locally important species.

People and Planet
Delivering responsible 
sourcing across our 
supply chain

Suppliers are categorised into low, medium, and high-risk 
groups based on factors such as country and sector risk, 
sustainability commitments, and adverse media alerts. 
Low-risk suppliers are automatically approved, while 
high-risk suppliers undergo additional review, with 
mitigation measures and action plans implemented where 
gaps are identified. This may be followed by on-site audits 
conducted by internal teams or third parties, with ongoing 
monitoring to ensure compliance with defined standards. 
Based on outcomes, we determine whether to continue, 
enhance, or terminate supplier relationships.

This includes the adoption by the European Commission of 
the revised European Sustainability Reporting Standards 
(ESRS) and the transposition of the revised EU texts into 
national law. Elsewhere internationally, some governments 
across the Asia-Pacific region are tightening sustainability 
regulation, whilst in other jurisdictions, notably the United 
States, the regulatory landscape is currently in a state of flux.

The governance of our supply chain is underpinned by 
our existing sustainability and responsibility policies and 
procedures, including Human Rights, Anti-Corruption, 
Conflict Minerals, Code of Business Conduct, Health and 
Safety and Environment. Together, these frameworks 
provide a consistent foundation for managing ESG risks and 
opportunities across our global operations and supply base.

Responsible Sourcing Due Diligence Procedure
Since January 2024, we have been implementing an 
enhanced Responsible Sourcing Due Diligence Procedure, 
which we have further strengthened with comprehensive 
environmental, social and governance criteria. It provides a 
structured framework for managing risks related to business 
partners and ensuring compliance with anti-corruption, 
anti-money laundering, economic sanctions, and ESG-
related requirements. Our strategy is based on a risk-based 
approach, recognising that not all suppliers present the 
same level of exposure.

Against this dynamic regulatory backdrop, we remain 
focused on strengthening our capacity, systems, and 
resources to respond effectively to existing, emerging, 
and future requirements as they become clearer. Proactive 
regulatory preparedness supports business continuity, 
reduces compliance risks and protects our reputation 
in increasingly transparent and regulated markets.

As a global steel and mining business, the sustainability 
of our operations depends heavily on the integrity and 
performance of our supply chain. Responsible sourcing is 
therefore not only an environmental or social expectation 
— it is a core driver of business resilience, regulatory 
readiness, and long-term competitiveness. The upstream 
value chain represents the most significant share of our 
sustainability impacts, exposures and opportunities, 
from human rights and environmental stewardship to 
compliance with evolving legislation.

In practical terms, our vendor management system is now 
integrated with the company’s risk and compliance tool, 
allowing suppliers to respond directly to ESG-related 
questions where risks have been identified. This enables 
more dynamic, targeted, and efficient risk assessment, 
supporting informed sourcing decisions and improved 
accountability across the supply chain.

We have developed an enhanced Responsible Sourcing 
Policy that outlines our approach and formalises our 
commitments to suppliers, complemented by an updated 
Code of Responsible Sourcing that sets out the standards 
we expect suppliers to meet. The policy applies across our 
operations and extends to suppliers, contractors, and 
their affiliates, covering all products and services procured 
by our company.

Suppliers undergo an initial screening, including 
detailed assessment across ESG topics, supported by 
market and risk intelligence. We now use real-time risk 
monitoring tools, including Dow Jones and Dun & Bradstreet 
risk and compliance platforms, which alert us to issues 
across human rights, environmental performance and 
financial integrity.

By actively engaging with suppliers and embedding clear, 
consistent standards across our value chain, we strengthen 
our licence to operate, reduce operational and reputational 
risks, meet the expectations of customers and regulators, 
and create a more secure, transparent, and future-ready 
supply base. This approach helps ensure continuity of 
access to strategic raw materials and inputs, while 
supporting safer working conditions, more responsible 
environmental practices, and greater traceability for 
customers and stakeholders.

The updated Responsible Sourcing Policy and revised Code 
are scheduled for launch in 2026, although many of their 
principles are already being applied in practice. They are 
designed to integrate sustainability considerations in line 
with our objectives, anticipated regulatory developments, 
and recognised international frameworks and standards, 
including the International Labour Organisation’s Tripartite 
Declaration of Principles concerning Multinational Enterprises 
and Social Policy. The update also reflects relevant 
responsible sourcing requirements from ResponsibleSteel™’s 
core site certification standard. ResponsibleSteel™’s Principle 
3 relates to the responsible sourcing of raw materials in steel 
production, and we encourage all our raw materials suppliers 
to align with this standard.

For our stakeholders, responsible sourcing delivers tangible 
value: clearer expectations and support for suppliers, 
improved transparency and traceability for customers, and 
enhanced risk management and resilience for investors.

Governance and evolving regulatory environment 
for supply chain responsibility
The regulatory environment for supply chain due diligence has 
continued to evolve in recent years, with legislation enacted at 
national level, including the German Supply Chain Due Diligence 
Act and the French Duty of Vigilance Law. At EU level, although 
revisions to the Corporate Sustainability Reporting Directive 
(CSRD) and the Corporate Sustainability Due Diligence 
Directive (CSDDD) have entered into force, the regulatory 
framework still needs to be clarified in several aspects.

In addition, the policy and Code introduce enhanced 
requirements for raw material suppliers, establish 
monitoring and oversight mechanisms, and outline potential 
measures where suppliers do not meet stated expectations. 
Suppliers are also expected to implement and promote 
these requirements throughout their own value chains, 
helping to cascade responsible practices beyond our direct 
business relationships.

 We will finalise and formally launch the Responsible

communicated and embedded across our organisation 
and supplier network. This will provide a consistent, 
group-wide framework for supply chain responsibility, 
taking into account emerging regulatory requirements 
such as CSRD and CSDDD, and recognised international 
standards. By setting clear expectations and 
accountability mechanisms, the policy and Code will 
support compliance at scale, improve supplier 
understanding of our requirements, and enable more 
robust and responsible sourcing decisions across regions 
and product categories.

For customers, it enhances transparency and confidence in

We launched a primary data collection process, requesting 
product carbon footprints and life-cycle assessment 
documentation, supported by a data validation framework. 
By the end of 2025, we had received cradle-to-gate data for

Knowledge building 
Contractor safety is a material topic for ArcelorMittal and 
a key focus of our three-year health and safety roadmap. 
Across our operations, contractor personnel numbers can

Engagement activities may include dialogue with senior 
management and compliance representatives, guidance 
on strengthening policies and management systems, 
and the development of mitigation measures with clear

capacity-building initiatives for supply chain workers.

One of the key areas of focus relates to safety awareness 
and the management of critical risks. In response, a

Engagement with higher-risk suppliers is intended

By working collaboratively with suppliers, we strengthen

3. 	 Deepening engagement and oversight of  
high-risk suppliers
	
 We will continue to prioritise targeted engagement and 
monitoring strategies for suppliers identified as higher risk 
from an ESG perspective, including mitigation plans, 
audits, follow-up reviews, and, where necessary, 
remediation actions. This focus reflects the fact that 
higher-risk suppliers represent the greatest potential 
exposure to human rights, environmental, reputational, 
and operational risks. Proactive engagement is intended 
to drive tangible improvements in supplier performance, 
prevent incidents before they occur, protect workers and 
communities, and reduce the likelihood of supply disruptions 
or reputational damage, thereby strengthening trust with 
customers, investors and regulators.

Priorities for 2026 
In 2026, our focus will be on embedding responsible sourcing 
more deeply into core business processes, moving from 
policy and frameworks toward consistent, measurable 
implementation across our global supply chain. These 
priorities are designed to strengthen regulatory readiness, 
improve transparency and data quality, and enhance the 
long-term resilience and sustainability of our supply base.

With regard to human rights, a dedicated training 
programme has been developed in collaboration with 
the compliance team, with a primary focus on higher-risk 
suppliers, to strengthen understanding of human rights risks 
and minimum standards.

Advancing supplier data quality for Scope 3 transparency
Improving the accuracy and completeness of Scope 3 
emissions data from our supply chain has been a clear 
priority in 2025, as it represents a significant share of our 
overall emissions profile and a key area of regulatory and 
stakeholder focus. Scope 3 is therefore an integral 
component of our responsible sourcing approach, linking 
supplier engagement, data transparency, and long-term 
decarbonisation objectives.

4.	 Strengthening regulatory and industry engagement
	
 We will pursue active dialogue with regulators, industry 
peers, and multi-stakeholder initiatives to contribute to 
the evolving policy landscape and remain aligned with 
emerging expectations. As supply chain regulation 
continues to develop rapidly, early and constructive 
engagement enables us to anticipate regulatory change, 
adapt our systems proactively, and influence practical 
implementation where possible. This supports regulatory 
compliance, reduces uncertainty and compliance risk, 
and helps ensure continuity of operations in an increasingly 
regulated global environment.

1.	 Advancing safety compliance across the supply chain
	
 We will further strengthen safety compliance across our 
supply chain, with a continued focus on contractors and 
higher-risk suppliers. Given the scale of contractor 
involvement in our operations, improving safety awareness 
is critical to preventing fatalities and serious injuries. 
Collaboration in strengthening supplier safety management 
helps protect people, reduce operational disruptions, 
safeguard our reputation, and reinforce a strong culture 
of responsibility across our extended workforce.

Within our upstream footprint, purchased materials and 
products account for the largest share of emissions. We 
have a long-term objective to transition toward primary 
data from suppliers to improve the accuracy of our emissions 
accounting and inform more sustainable procurement 
decisions. Through a structured scoping exercise, we 
identified priority product categories — including iron ore, 
solid fuels, ferroalloys, base metals and industrial gases —

 Together, these priorities support our ambition to build 
a supply chain that is not only more responsible, but also 
more resilient, transparent and future-proof, delivering 
long-term value for suppliers, customers, investors, and 
the communities in which we operate.

Governance
Reinforcing business 
conduct and compliance

This directly responds to our corporate commitment,

This provides senior management and the Board with

Although no major policy revisions were issued in 2025, the 
year focused on accelerating implementation, ensuring 
employees understood the updated requirements, and 
improving processes to embed these standards more

to operational embedding reflects our focus on translating 
governance commitments into real behavioural change.

The guidance sets out a six-step process and how these 
should be embedded into each entity’s operations:

The Code of Business Conduct represents the core of 
the governance framework, and it serves as the guiding 
document for ethical decision-making and responsible 
practices throughout the company. It is applicable to all 
employees, directors, and officers of ArcelorMittal S.A. 
and subsidiaries worldwide, ensuring a consistent baseline 
of ethical conduct across all regions and business units. 
All employees must complete training on its principles within 
one month of hiring. Refresher training is required every 
three years to ensure alignment with evolving risks and the 
regulatory environment. More than 127,280 employees have 
been trained over the latest three-year cycle. This high level 
of coverage strengthens our ability to prevent misconduct, 
embed ethical decision-making, and ensure that employees 
understand how our standards apply in practice.

Responsibility and integrity underpin all aspects of 
ArcelorMittal’s business conduct and governance. They 
are central to our culture, our reputation, and our licence 
to operate. Against a rapidly evolving global regulatory 
environment, we focus on ensuring that our governance 
and compliance framework is effectively implemented, 
consistently applied and capable of addressing both current 
and emerging risks. To reinforce the materiality of this 
approach, strong governance practices are essential given 
the scale of our global footprint, the diversity of regulatory 
environments in which we operate, and the increasing 
expectations of stakeholders for transparent and responsible 
conduct. Our governance systems therefore play a critical role 
in safeguarding our licence to operate, managing regulatory 
exposure, and supporting sustainable value creation.

1.	  Embedding human rights issues into governance
2.	  Identifying and assessing human rights impacts 
3.	  Implementing management measures to prevent,  
cease, and mitigate adverse impacts
4.	 Tracking implementation
5.	 Communicating on how impacts are addressed 
6.	 Providing remediation.

Guidance on conducting human rights due diligence
In 2025, we launched comprehensive guidance on 
conducting human rights due diligence. The aim is to 
provide all ArcelorMittal entities with a structured and 
effective process to identify, prevent, mitigate, and remedy 
adverse human rights impacts in their own operations.

## CASE STUDY

Employees also participate in training programmes 
relevant to their roles and responsibilities, covering topics 
such as insider dealing regulations, human rights, economic 
sanctions, anti-trust, anti-corruption, and data protection. 
These trainings must be completed within three months of 
hiring, with refresher courses required every three years. 
Completion rates for these trainings remain high across 
regions, demonstrating strong engagement, and reflecting 
the increasing importance of compliance capabilities 
in a complex regulatory environment.

Conducting human rights due diligence (HRDD)  
at ArcelorMittal Andrade, Brazil

Strong governance supports the proactive identification 
and management of business conduct risks, while enabling 
informed decision-making aligned with our ethical 
standards and sustainability objectives. In 2025, our 
emphasis remained on embedding policies and procedures, 
strengthening systems, and building capabilities across 
our company. These actions directly respond to stakeholder 
expectations for transparency, accountability, and effective 
risk management.

Between February and July 2025, ArcelorMittal Andrade, 
Brazil conducted HRDD in line with the Human Rights Due 
Diligence Guidelines launched by the company. The 
objective was to identify and assess actual and potential 
human rights impacts associated with the operation, and 
to define appropriate follow-up actions where needed.

representatives identified through a stakeholder 
mapping process. In total, 170 people participated 
directly in the assessment: 93 survey respondents, 
62 external stakeholder interviews, and 15 interviews with 
internal leaders and managers. The team also conducted 
22 technical field visits. Potentially vulnerable and 
affected groups were identified through stakeholder 
mapping, taking into account protected characteristics, 
and were included in the engagement activities.

In addition to the Code of Business Conduct, we 
have developed several other compliance policies and 
procedures in more specific areas, such as human rights, 
anti-corruption, anti-money laundering, economic 
sanctions, antitrust, insider dealing, data protection, 
third-party due diligence, whistleblowing and grievance 
processes. Together, these policies form a comprehensive 
control framework to address key business conduct risks 
across our operations and value chain.

The assessment was conducted in four phases:  
planning and project management, regulatory 
analysis and initial impact screening, identification and 
assessment of impacts through desk research, interviews, 
surveys, and technical visits, and sharing of results with 
stakeholders, together with prioritised recommendations 
for management. This final phase will be concluded later 
this year.

Aligning business conduct policies 
with international standards
We have implemented a comprehensive approach for 
identifying, assessing, managing, and remediating material 
risks related to business conduct. This approach is embedded 
within our governance framework and underpinned by key 
policies such as the Code of Business Conduct and the 
Human Rights Policy. These policies draw on internationally 
recognised standards, including the UN Guiding Principles 
on Business and Human Rights and the OECD Guidelines 
for Multinational Enterprises. This helps us identify salient 
risks earlier, apply a consistent risk management approach 
across our global operations and value chain, and 
strengthen accountability for responsible business practices.

Initial findings identified opportunities to strengthen 
performance, and a follow-up study is underway 
to deepen the analysis and determine whether 
additional measures are required.

This case illustrates how the company’s HRDD 
guidance can be operationalised at site level, 
embedding structured assessment, stakeholder 
engagement, and continuous improvement 
into day-to-day operations.

Stakeholder engagement was a central element of 
the process. Participants included direct employees 
and contractors; residents of João Monlevade, Bela Vista 
de Minas, and Itabira; human rights and environmental 
organisations; municipal authorities across multiple 
departments; NGOs; schools; unions; and other

Our compliance programme involves regular reviews to help 
ensure policies remain reflective of evolving legal, ethical, 
and operating standards and best practices. There is a 
semi-annual compliance certification process covering all 
business segments and entailing reporting to the audit and 
risk committee that oversees business conduct governance.

*Page 44*


ArcelorMittal Sustainability Report 2025

## Governance – Reinforcing business conduct and compliance continued

Strengthening our responsible sourcing
In response to the evolving regulatory landscape, we 
continue to strengthen our governance of responsible 
sourcing through the refinement of our Responsible Sourcing 
Policy and related Code, both scheduled for formal launch 
in 2026 and already being embedded in practice. These 
updates enhance oversight, accountability, and 
consistency in managing sustainability and human rights 
risks across our supply chain, while aligning our approach 
with emerging regulatory requirements and recognised 
international standards.

In addition, a new compliance testing tool will be adopted 
to strengthen oversight and consistency. This tool will enable 
the systematic tracking of deficiencies and the harmonisation 
of monitoring KPIs across the company, improving visibility of 
compliance risks, supporting more consistent performance 
monitoring, and enabling more data-driven prioritisation 
of remediation actions.

Implementation of Whistleblower Policy  
and Grievance Procedure
We updated our Whistleblower Policy in 2024 in light of the 
EU Whistleblowing Directive and the US Sarbanes-Oxley 
Act. It now has a much broader scope than previously, and 
covers human rights, health and safety, climate and 
the environment, labour practices, and data protection. 
Our Whistleblower policy is supported by a comprehensive 
grievance reporting and management system that was further 
enhanced to take account of regulatory developments.

There has been a further focus on training for our local 
grievance officers and investigators. In June 2025, group 
compliance launched a series of training modules for local 
non-forensic grievance investigators (including 
professionals from health & safety, environment, human 
resources, and community relations). Delivered by an 
external provider and recorded for ongoing use, the training 
was designed to build long-term investigation capabilities 
across the organisation. Its strategic objective was to 
enhance the consistency, quality, and credibility of local 
investigations, recognising that ineffective grievance 
handling can erode stakeholder trust, increase legal and 
regulatory exposure, and result in the escalation of issues 
beyond the company level. We observed an increase in the 
maturity of case handling, with clearer escalation pathways 
and more systematic documentation supporting better 
quality outcomes for reporters and affected stakeholders.

Together, these upgrades will strengthen our governance 
effectiveness by enhancing transparency, improving 
real-time visibility over compliance risks, and supporting 
more systematic and consistent oversight across the group. 
At the same time, AI-enabled tools will help embed compliance 
more seamlessly into everyday business processes, making 
it easier for employees to understand their obligations, 
access guidance when needed, and apply policies 
correctly, thereby reinforcing a strong and sustainable 
culture of compliance.

Employees, contractors, and third parties can report 
concerns via secure and accessible channels such as an 
online portal, dedicated phone hotlines in various countries, 
and direct communication with management, HR, legal, 
or compliance representatives. Upon receipt, reported 
concerns are assessed by local grievance committees. 
Based on their materiality and criticality, high-risk concerns 
are promptly escalated to corporate oversight functions, 
while lower-risk concerns are assigned to local investigators.

We have also further developed our Responsible Sourcing 
Due Diligence Procedure by integrating enhanced ESG 
criteria and strengthening system capabilities, including 
the linkage between vendor management and risk and 
compliance tools. This enables a more dynamic, risk-based 
approach to supplier assessment and supports more 
effective oversight of higher-risk suppliers. Further detail 
on implementation and supplier engagement is provided 
in chapter 10.

By establishing a common investigation baseline and 
shared methodological standards, the initiative supports 
our risk management processes, and promotes alignment 
across business units operating in multiple jurisdictions. 
This approach contributes to early issue identification, 
fair outcomes, and timely remediation, reinforcing trust 
with employees, communities, and other stakeholders.

Investigations are conducted in accordance with the 
Grievance Procedure, ensuring independence, confidentiality, 
and compliance with ArcelorMittal’s standards. Investigation 
outcomes may result in remedial actions, such as disciplinary 
measures, contractual adjustments, workplace 
reinstatements, financial compensation for damages, and 
the implementation of new controls to prevent recurrence.

Priorities for 2026
During 2026, the corporate compliance team will work 
closely with local compliance teams to support the effective 
implementation of policies and procedures across the 
business, alongside continued upskilling and training to 
strengthen compliance capabilities. These actions will help 
ensure a more harmonised compliance framework across 
all regions and improve our ability to detect, assess, and 
address risks consistently, while strengthening first-line 
ownership of compliance across the organisation.

The programme consists of seven modules covering 
key elements of effective grievance management: 
(i) the Grievance Procedure, including principles and roles; 
(ii) whistleblowing reports; (iii) best practices for conducting 
interviews; (iv) document review; (v) analysis of findings and 
investigation reporting; (vi) remedial and corrective actions; 
and (vii) environmental investigations.

In 2025, our focus was on implementation of the policy 
and the procedure and process improvement. During the 
year, the reporting and follow-up grievance procedures 
were tested, monitored and refined across the business. 
This has been a learning process, helping us to better 
understand the nature and materiality of reported cases, 
determine when escalation or external counsel is required, 
and support effective remediation and resolution. 
This has helped us strengthen consistency in case handling, 
with clearer escalation pathways and more systematic 
documentation supporting better outcomes for reporters 
and affected stakeholders.

In parallel, we will further invest in IT systems to improve 
operational efficiency and accessibility. This includes 
enhancement of key compliance tools, such as the conflict 
of interest tool, and the rollout of the upgraded Group Policy 
and Procedure Management (GPPM) system. GPPM will 
provide employees with real-time access to up-to-date 
policies and procedures, supported by embedded AI 
functionality, including a chatbot to assist with routine 
compliance-related queries. This will improve employee 
accessibility to compliance guidance, reduce reliance on 
manual support from compliance teams, and lower the 
practical burden of navigating complex policy frameworks 
in day-to-day operations.

A total of 250 employees acting as investigators across 
all business units were invited to participate in the English 
language training. This initiative represents an important step 
in embedding a strong speak up culture and ensuring that 
grievances are addressed in a transparent, consistent, and 
accountable manner across the company. The modules are 
now available via our SharePoint platform for investigators to 
revisit online, and for new joiners to be onboarded efficiently.

Governance
Governance structures 
and risk management

Appointments, Remuneration and Corporate Governance 
Committee (ARCGC) 
The ARCGC reviews and approves corporate goals and 
objectives relevant to the executive officers and senior 
management’s compensation and evaluates performance 
considering these goals. It makes recommendations to 
the Board with respect to trends in Board remuneration, 
incentive compensation plans and equity-based 
incentive plans.

## The Board of Directors has three committees:

Following a comprehensive third-party health and safety 
audit completed in 2024, our health and safety assurance 
model has been strengthened by introducing three lines 
of assurance across all business units.

## – Audit and Risk Committee

## – Appointments, Remuneration, and Corporate Governance

Committee (ARCG Committee) 
	
– Sustainability Committee.

The Audit and Risk Committee receives regular quarterly 
reports from the chief information officer (CIO) and the 
chief information security officer (CISO) on the company’s 
cybersecurity risk profile and enterprise cybersecurity 
programme and meets with the CIO and CISO at 
least quarterly.

For more information on the composition and remit of each 
committee, visit the ArcelorMittal website.

Strong corporate governance, integrity, and oversight are 
fundamental to the company’s long-term performance, 
reputation and value. Effective governance, risk management, 
and oversight are also essential to delivering on our 
sustainability commitments and ensuring compliance with 
evolving legal, regulatory and ethical standards globally. 
They are critical to maintaining the trust and confidence of 
all our stakeholders – from customers and local communities 
to our value chain partners, investors and employees.

The Committee also identifies candidates qualified to serve 
as members of the Board and the Executive Officers and 
recommends candidates to the Board for appointment by 
the general meeting of shareholders or for appointment by 
the Board to fulfil interim vacancies on the Board.

## Management Committees and Panels include:

Each year, the Audit and Risk Committee reviews and 
recommends the company’s Information Security Policy and 
Information Security Programme to the Board for approval. 
At least annually, the Board reviews and discusses the 
company’s technology strategy with the CIO and approves 
the company’s strategic technology plan.

## – Management Committee

## – Corporate Finance and Tax Committee (CFTC)

## – Investment Allocation Committee (IAC)

## – Global Health and Safety Council

The ARCGC develops, monitors, and reviews corporate 
governance principles applicable to the company. 
It facilitates the evaluation of the Board and reviews the 
succession planning and the executive development 
programme for the members of the executive officers, 
and reviews analysis provided by proxy advisory firms 
in the context of corporate governance compensation. 
It also reviews relevant policies and procedures relating 
to compliance and governance, reviews the global findings 
of employee surveys, reports conclusions to the Board, 
and makes recommendations for approval.

## – Climate Change Panel

– Sustainable Development Panel.

In this chapter, we bring together three complementary 
elements of our sustainability governance framework: 
our governance structures, our enterprise risk management 
approach, and our approach to industry-leading external 
standards. Together, these mechanisms ensure that our 
policies and commitments are not only defined and 
overseen at group level, but are also consistently 
implemented, monitored, and assured in practice across our 
operations. In particular, third-party certification through 
ResponsibleSteel™ plays an important role in translating our 
governance and risk management systems into verified 
site-level performance, providing independent assurance 
that our policies, procedures, and controls are effectively 
embedded and delivering outcomes on the ground.

The Audit and Risk Committee also examines the annual, 
half-year and quarterly financial statements for the 
parent company and the group and provides commentary 
on the accounting principles, valuation methods, and 
reporting rules.

There have been no significant changes to governance 
committees or their responsibilities during 2025.

From a governance standpoint, accountability starts at site 
and segment level. Operational units are responsible for 
identifying and managing sustainability related risks and 
opportunities as part of their day to day activities. At group 
level, functions and oversight bodies review issues in 
aggregate, set policies and standards, and provide the 
frameworks, tools, and expert guidance that support 
consistent implementation across the business.

The Committee is composed of four independent directors, 
appointed directly by the Board. It meets at least four times 
per year and may convene additional meetings at the 
request of at least two of its members.

The Board of Directors Committees
Audit and Risk Committee 
The Audit and Risk Committee assists the Board of Directors 
(the Board) in fulfilling its oversight responsibilities by 
reviewing: the integrity of the financial reports and other 
financial information; ArcelorMittal’s compliance with legal 
and regulatory requirements; the registered public 
accounting firm’s (independent auditor) qualifications and 
independence; systems of internal control regarding 
finance, accounting, legal compliance, ethics, and risk 
management that the Board and the executive officers 
have established; auditing, accounting, and financial 
reporting processes generally; the identification and 
management of risks to which ArcelorMittal is exposed to; 
overseeing cybersecurity risk, information security, and 
technology risk as well as management’s actions to identify, 
assess, mitigate, and remediate material issues.

Governance structures 
ArcelorMittal’s governance structure is based on the 
following supervisory bodies:

The Board of Directors: It is composed of nine directors, five 
of whom are independent. It is responsible for the strategic 
direction and oversight of the business, as well as the 
appointment of senior management.

The Board believes it has the right mix of skills, knowledge, 
experience and perspectives to govern the business 
effectively. Its composition is reviewed regularly, with 
additional capabilities sought as needed to support 
ArcelorMittal’s evolving business. Further details can be 
found in the Annual Report, page 137.

## Governance – Governance structures and risk management continued

Sustainable Development Panel (SDP)
The SDP consists of senior managers from relevant corporate 
functions and key operations. It discusses, coordinates 
and guides engagement on issues related to material 
environmental and social issues, stakeholder engagement, 
and performance on environmental management, human 
rights, and social performance issues. The SDP meets on an 
ad hoc as required basis.

The ARCGC meetings are convened by the Chair at least 
four times per year and may also be held at the request of 
at least two members. Members of the Committee possess 
the expertise and experience relevant to its mandate. 
The Chair provides a verbal report to the Board after 
each meeting summarising the Committee’s discussions, 
decisions and findings.

Sustainability Committee (SC) 
The SC comprises three members, of whom two are 
independent, appointed by the Board. It guides 
management on health, safety, climate, environmental 
and community policies, including catastrophic risks like 
tailings management.

Executive-level committees and panels 
Management Committee (MC) 
The MC plays an important role in debating and developing 
the group’s policies and strategy and brings to the table 
representatives from all major segments, regions, and 
functions to discuss relevant matters for the group. It meets 
at least four times a year. For more information see the 
ArcelorMittal website.

It ensures sustainability reporting in the Annual Report and 
other public documents is adequate and monitors relevant 
legal actions. The SC may conduct independent investigations 
within its scope and ensures representation at the Annual 
General Meeting to address sustainability questions.

The ARCGC is composed of at least three independent 
directors appointed by the Board.

Global Health and Safety Council 
The Global Health and Safety Council – comprising Health 
and Safety (H&S) leaders from across the company – 
facilitates the sharing of learnt lessons and best practices, 
is consulted on policies, manuals and standards, as well as 
key lessons learned from incidents, and promotes our One 
Safety Culture and governance. It meets monthly virtually 
and convenes at one of our sites once a year. Once a 
quarter, there is an additional council meeting extended 
to COOs and CEOs.

Group CTO Global Technology Roadmap Panel 
The Group CTO Panel coordinates and monitors progress on 
the global technology roadmap, including decarbonisation 
activities, through regional and project-based levels involving 
the Corporate Technology Office (CTO) and research and 
development (R&D) functions.

The SC meetings are convened by the Chair at least four times 
per year and may also be held at the request of at least two 
members. Members of the Committee possess the expertise 
and experience relevant to its mandate. The Chair provides 
a verbal report to the Board after each meeting summarising 
the Committee’s discussions, decisions and findings.

XCarb® Technical Panel 
The XCarb® Technical Panel reviews requests from 
the network to have their products or projects appear 
beneath the XCarb® brand in accordance with its strict 
technical criteria.

Corporate Finance and Tax Committee (CFTC) 
The CFTC defines the financial management principles for 
ArcelorMittal’s finance teams. It also develops and supports 
financial and business solutions for the group by providing 
expertise, operational excellence, and stability, enabling 
the company’s sustainable and competitive growth while 
developing and promoting the capabilities of its community. 
The responsibilities of the CFTC extend across all finance 
and tax activities in the group, including treasury, funding, 
taxation, accounting, and performance management, 
Sarbanes-Oxley compliance and insurance. The CFTC 
is chaired by the CFO and EVP, Mr Genuino Christino.

Risk management 
We apply a disciplined and forward-looking approach to 
risk management across the enterprise to identify, assess, 
and manage threats to our people, assets, and operations. 
It is regarded as part of our wider corporate governance 
structure to protect and build the value of the business.

Management is responsible for internal controls and has 
implemented on an ongoing basis a robust management 
and control system covering short-, medium-, and long-term 
risks, including ESG and climate-related risks. This is designed 
to ensure the business is focused on achieving its objectives 
and that significant risks are identified and mitigated. The 
process consists of risk identification, risk analysis, inherent 
risk evaluation, risk treatment, and residual risk evaluation.

Investment Allocations Committee (IAC) 
The IAC authorises large capex projects, including those 
designed to support safety, reliability, and environmental 
improvements and reviews the carbon reductions footprint 
of all proposals. The IAC is chaired by Mr Aditya Mittal, 
CEO of ArcelorMittal.

ArcelorMittal’s risk management and internal control system 
is designed to determine risks in relation to the achievement 
of business objectives and appropriate risk responses.

Climate Change Panel (CCP) 
The CCP consists of senior managers from relevant corporate 
functions and key operations across the company. It guides 
engagement and advocacy with external stakeholders on 
climate change and decarbonisation and supports the 
business in understanding the risks and opportunities 
associated with the transition to a low-carbon economy. 
The CCP typically meets on a quarterly basis.

## Governance – Governance structures and risk management continued

Aligning with industry-leading standards
ResponsibleSteel™ core site certification is a rigorous, 
independent assurance process that evaluates a steel site’s 
operations against comprehensive environmental, social, 
and governance management criteria. Certification is 
awarded for a three-year period at a time, and is subject 
to ongoing oversight, including regular surveillance audits, 
the timely resolution of any non-conformities, and clear 
evidence of continuous improvement in order to maintain 
certified status.

The establishment and maintenance of a risk identification 
and management process is the responsibility of site/
segment/corporate function management.

## CASE STUDY

ResponsibleSteel™ certification and continuous 
improvement at ArcelorMittal Luxembourg

Risks are owned and monitored by management. Risk 
officers designated by management facilitate the 
conversations and help monitor action plans. Critical  
risks are escalated through existing reporting lines.

ResponsibleSteel™ certification continues to serve as a 
driver of continuous improvement at ArcelorMittal Belval 
and Differdange, Luxembourg. Since the initial 
certification audit in 2021, the sites have embedded 
the ResponsibleSteel™ standard into their management 
systems and day-to-day operations, using the framework 
to strengthen governance, stakeholder engagement 
and environmental stewardship.

A key focus area has been the systematic identification 
and management of local stakeholder expectations 
and environmental risks. Over time, the sites have 
strengthened their stakeholder engagement approach, 
clarifying internal responsibilities and introducing 
more structured mechanisms to capture, prioritise 
and respond to stakeholder feedback. This approach 
is now embedded as part of routine governance and 
performance review processes.

Global Assurance – Group Risk Management facilitates 
the risk management process by providing the appropriate 
framework to risk owners (i.e. business units/segments/
clusters/business divisions/corporate functions), a risk 
management platform for reporting, a training academy 
to develop competencies and improve risk culture, and 
by supporting the implementation of Enterprise Risk 
Management across the group. A consolidated report 
is facilitated on a half-yearly basis and shared with the 
key stakeholders.

Achieving and retaining certification requires sustained 
engagement across multiple functions, including 
operations, health and safety, human resources, 
procurement, environmental management, and 
sustainability. Certified sites must demonstrate robust 
management systems, effective stakeholder engagement, 
and measurable progress in areas such as occupational 
safety, labour practices, environmental performance, 
and climate transition.

The initial certification audit identified several areas 
for improvement, which were addressed through a 
structured and phased action plan. Rather than treating 
these findings as one-off compliance gaps, the sites 
integrated the required measures into existing 
management processes. Subsequent surveillance audits 
and the most recent recertification audit have provided 
regular opportunities to evaluate progress, test the 
effectiveness of implemented actions and identify 
further enhancements.

The certification process also highlighted the 
importance of maintaining a consistent, data-driven 
understanding of local environmental impacts. In 
response, the sites conducted targeted assessments 
to refine their understanding of site-specific risks and 
to inform mitigation strategies. These assessments now 
underpin ongoing monitoring programmes, ensuring 
that environmental management actions remain 
proportionate, evidence-based and responsive 
to changing conditions.

The Audit and Risk Committee assists the Board with the 
oversight of risks to which ArcelorMittal is exposed and in 
monitoring and reviewing the risk management framework 
and process.

By end of 2025, 42 of our sites had achieved 
ResponsibleSteel™ certification. While no new sites 
were certified during the year, 19 sites were successfully 
recertified, four sites underwent recertification audits, 
one site completed an initial certification audit, and 
18 sites were subject to surveillance audits during 2025.

These surveillance and recertification activities illustrate 
the ongoing nature of the ResponsibleSteel™ certification 
cycle and provide evidence of continuous improvement 
across our certified sites. The volume and frequency of 
audits undertaken during the year highlight both the scale 
of operational coordination required and our commitment 
to maintaining certification through sustained performance, 
rather than relying on legacy status.

Environmental stewardship planning has likewise evolved. 
Baselines have been established in priority areas, 
supported by action plans that are reviewed periodically 
and updated to reflect operational developments, 
regulatory expectations and stakeholder input. 
This structured review cycle ensures that improvement 
measures remain relevant and aligned with best practice.

Today, ResponsibleSteel™ certification continues to 
function as an active governance mechanism rather than 
a static milestone. Through regular audits, internal reviews 
and stakeholder dialogue, ArcelorMittal Belval and 
Differdange maintain a dynamic improvement cycle that 
reinforces accountability, strengthens risk management 
and supports resilient, long-term sustainability outcomes 
at site level.

The full list of our certified sites can be found on the 
ResponsibleSteel™ website.
